| `setting` present, first wording (`shell` said "Prefer it for settings") | 12, none to `setting` | 13 | 55,498 | volume 9, percentage on (dark theme not read) |
| `setting` present, wording as shipped, three runs | 2, 2, 2 | 3, 3, 3 | 12,626, 15,959, 15,971 | all three settings changed (dark theme read in the third run only) |
| `setting` disallowed | 1 | 2 | 5,816 | volume still 5; the agent said it had set it |

The first run is why the `shell` description no longer says to prefer it for settings: the agent never tried `setting`, and spent ten calls on the volume because `cmd media_session volume --set` changes nothing on API 37. The control run failed the same way and reported success. Five runs, one task, one model: a direction, not a rate.

**Cost.** The tool adds 482 characters to what a model reads of the tool list on every turn (15 tools, 4,596 characters). A search answers in up to 15 lines.

**Limits.** Android 17 only, and only where the shell user may run app functions: seen on a userdebug emulator image, not on a retail phone. The other packages with the same functions (Play services, Digital Wellbeing) are not indexed. Values are the device's strings, passed through. The sensitivity mark `MUST_PROVIDE_UNDO` (airplane mode, Bluetooth, the hotspot; 4 settings) is not acted on.

**Reopen if.** A retail Android 17 phone refuses the shell user; `getDeviceStateItem` starts working (a read becomes one call); third-party apps publish functions worth a general tool.

## D58. A crash is said in the reply of the action that caused it, and `logs` reads the app's lines

**Choice.** After an action that ends on a new screen (any action but `launch` and `open`), the daemon asks the device whether the app that was in front crashed since the action began, and a crash goes into the reply: `crashed: <app> stopped during this action: <exception> at <frame>`, with a hint naming `logs`. `logs` is a CLI verb, an MCP tool and a daemon method: the last 40 lines the app logged in the last 60 seconds, its last crash first. Android only. The crash buffer is read with `logcat -b crash -d -T <device time>` through the agent's shell; an app's lines are picked with `--uid`, because the pid is gone once it has crashed.

**Why.** The people who file issues on these tools are developers testing their own app (the roadmap research, 2026-11-15), and logs are what they ask for next after screenshots; mobile-mcp and agent-device have them. For an agent, a crash looked like any other screen change: the launcher, or the app's earlier screen after Android restarts it. The system's "keeps stopping" dialog shows only from the second crash. Saying it in the reply costs the agent no call and no decision to look.

**Rejected.** Reading the crash log only when the app in front changed: Android often restarts the app on its previous screen after a crash, which is the case an agent cannot see. A check on every action: most actions change part of a screen and cannot be a crash that the agent would miss. Logs as an `apps` action: hints name the verb (`phonedriver logs`), and a hint must name something that exists as a tool.

**Evidence.** API 37 emulator, 2026-11-15. With `am crash dev.phonedriver.testbed` timed to fire during a tap, the tap answered `ok tapped @e6 "Busy" ... new screen`, the system's `alert android: "phonedriver testbed keeps stopping"`, and `crashed: dev.phonedriver.testbed stopped during this action: android.app.RemoteServiceException$CrashedByAdbException: shell-induced crash at android.app.ActivityThread.throwRemoteServiceException(ActivityThread.java:2581)`. `logs dev.phonedriver.testbed --grep AndroidRuntime` printed the same crash and the trace. The planted crash is the system's own (`am crash`), not an exception thrown by the app's code on a tap: the testbed has no button that crashes.

**Cost.** The read took 70.7, 73.4, 79.2 and 99.8 ms on the emulator, on the actions that showed a new screen (their settle and tree reads took 0.8 to 2.9 s). An action that changes part of a screen, or nothing, pays nothing. The `logs` tool adds 219 characters to the tool list (16 tools, 4,815 characters).

**Limits.** Java and Kotlin crashes only: a native crash writes a tombstone, not a `FATAL EXCEPTION` block, and an ANR is not a crash. Only the app that was in front before the action is checked. `--uid` was tried on API 37 only. iOS has no implementation: the Simulator's `log show` and a device's crash reports are not wired.

**Reopen if.** The read shows up in the speed benchmark (then the agent keeps one crash-buffer reader open and answers from memory); a testbed button that throws lets the app's own frame be proven; someone asks for iOS.

## D59. `record` writes the stream the daemon already receives into an MP4, with the actions beside it

**Choice.** `phonedriver record start` and `record stop` (daemon method `record`; in MCP the `record` argument of `screenshot`) save a video of the screen: the H.264 access units the Android agent already sends, written into an MP4 file by a writer in `crates/video` (`record.rs`, 337 lines, no new dependency). Nothing is decoded or encoded again. The file starts at the first keyframe after the start, which `record start` asks the encoder for (since 0.1.7, D107; before, it started with every unit kept since the last keyframe, which held screens from before the start). While a recording runs, each successful action appends `{"t_ms", "summary"}` to `<file>.steps.jsonl`: the time on the video's clock and the summary line of the action's reply.

**Why.** A recording is what a developer attaches to a bug report and what a person watches to see what an agent did; it ranks sixth in what users of these tools ask for (the roadmap research, 2026-11-15), and mobile-mcp, Pinky and agent-device have one. The daemon holds the encoded stream anyway, so a recording costs a file write per frame and no second encoder on the phone, which is what `adb shell screenrecord` would add (with its three-minute limit and a file to pull afterwards). The steps file is what makes the video usable by an agent or a script: it says at which second each action began, in words the action's own reply used.

**Rejected.** `screenrecord` on the device: a second encoder next to ours, a time limit, and nothing on iOS either. An MP4 crate: the writer needs seven box types and one track, and the crates on offer bring their own H.264 parsing or an async runtime. Timing frames by the encoder's time stamps alone: on the API 37 emulator's software encoder the stamps ran ahead of the clock (0.6 s over a 5 s recording; frames repeated for an idle screen are stamped exactly 100 ms apart), so the video outlasted its recording and ran ahead of the steps file. A picture is now placed by the gap between stamps and never later than the moment the daemon read it. Asking the encoder for a keyframe at the start instead of keeping the last group: an idle screen encodes nothing, so the keyframe would not come until something moved (reversed in 0.1.7, D107: the kept group put screens from up to 38 s before the start into the file). A seventeenth MCP tool: recording is rare, and every tool's text is read on every model turn; the argument on `screenshot` costs 155 characters.

**Evidence.** API 37 emulator through the win-pc tunnel, daemon on the Mac mini (debug build), 2026-11-15. A recording across `launch settings`, a tap that found nothing, `press back` and `scroll down` with pauses between them: `15.2 s, 1080x2424, 150 frames, 3.6 MB`; the wall clock between the two commands was 15.14 to 15.24 s; `ffprobe` reports `codec_name=h264`, `duration=15.180356`, `nb_frames=150`; `ffmpeg` decodes all of it and re-encodes it to 30 frames per second (455 frames, 15.17 s). The steps file has the three actions that succeeded, at 14, 6404 and 11119 ms; frames taken every 2 s show the home screen, Settings at 2 and 4 s, the home screen from 6 s and the app drawer after the scroll at 12 s. An earlier recording caught what the replies only hinted at: Settings sat on its splash screen for 30 s on a cold emulator, which is why two actions failed. Before the two timing fixes, the same kind of run gave 25.3 s of video for 24.2 s of wall clock (the reader was 1.0 s behind at the start) and 5.5 s for 4.85 s (stamps ahead of the clock). Tests: the writer's output is parsed back box by box and its samples decode with openh264 to the fixture's 59 frames (`crates/video/tests/record.rs`); a recording started mid-stream decodes from its first sample and is as long as it ran; the daemon's side is `crates/cli/tests/record.rs`.

**Cost.** At a start, the cached group goes into the file: 15, 83 and 256 units (0.3, 0.7 and 2.4 MB) in three runs, each lasting 11 microseconds of video. The daemon holds that group in memory for every connected Android device, recording or not; it is bounded by the encoder's keyframe interval (10 s at 60 frames per second). The tool list is 4,970 characters (16 tools; it was 4,815).

**Limits.** No sound. Android only: iOS tiers answer `unsupported`. A turned screen changes the stream's size and ends the recording; `stop` says so. The file is written on the daemon's machine. A daemon killed before `record stop` leaves a file without its index, which players cannot open; a dropped connection or an expired lease finishes the file. A secure window is black in the video, as it is in a screenshot. Typed text is never in the steps file, but a password field shown in clear on screen is in the video. Not tried on a physical phone; its hardware encoder may stamp frames differently, which the placement rule allows for. The time stamps were compared with the clock on one emulator only.

**Reopen if.** A phone's encoder never sends a second keyframe (then the cached group grows without bound and the start must ask for a keyframe instead); someone needs a recording to survive a turned screen; someone asks for iOS (the CoreDevice tier's stream is H.264 too).

## D60. `save` writes what a session did as steps, and `replay` runs them again with no model

**Choice.** Every session keeps a journal of the calls that succeeded (`act`, `wait`, `shell`, `app`, and a `setting` that wrote or opened). `phonedriver save <file>` writes it as one JSON object a line and empties it. `phonedriver replay <file> [--from N]` sends the lines again in order, one call each, and stops at the first step that fails, with the steps that ran, the screen as it is, and the number of the step to continue from. In MCP both are the `replay` tool (`save: true`). A ref in a target is saved as the element's text and role when they single it out on the screen the agent had seen, and as its centre otherwise, marked `by_position`. Each action is saved with whether it changed the screen; a replayed step that changed it then and changes nothing now is a miss. A typed secret and a shell command given with `--secret` are saved as `manual` steps with no text: a replay stops there.

**Why.** The first use of these tools is testing one's own app (the roadmap research, 2026-11-15), which means doing the same path again and again; a model turn per step is the slow and costly part, and MobileGPT measured 62.5% less latency on warm runs (one source). The research also warns that shortcuts an agent writes down without checking succeed 10% of the time (MAS-Bench, one source), so nothing is kept that was not seen to work: only calls that succeeded enter the journal, with the target the daemon resolved, not what the agent meant. A step that misses hands exactly that step to the model: the reply has the screen and the step number, so the model does one step and replays the rest.

**Rejected.** Saving refs: `@e16` means something only within one session. Saving every target as a point: a list one row longer breaks it silently. A replay inside the daemon as one `batch`: `batch` runs actions and waits only, and a run that clears an app's data with `shell` first would replay without it. The steps file as command lines (`tap "Internet"`): it reads better, but it needs a writer that is the exact inverse of the command parser for every action, and a step is what the daemon ran, so JSON is exact. Checking the diff each step produced, line by line: clocks, counts and loading states make equal runs differ; whether the screen changed is the part that held in every run.

**Evidence.** API 37 emulator, daemon on the Mac mini (debug build), 2026-11-15. A seven-step flow in Settings, done by ref from snapshots (`launch settings --restart`, tap Network & internet, tap Internet, back, back, `scroll down --until "Display & touch"`, tap it), then `save`: `saved 7 steps`, and the file's targets read `{"role":"button","text":"Network & internet"}`. From the home screen, `replay` five times: `replayed 7 steps in 18.9 s`, 19.3, 17.5, 18.2, 18.1, each ending on the Display & touch screen (the `Dark theme` rows in the printed screen). With step 3 edited to a label that does not exist: `step 3 of 7 (tap "Intranet") failed: no element matches "Intranet"`, the two steps that ran, the Network & internet screen, exit 1; after `tap "Internet"` by hand, `replay --from 4` printed `replayed 4 steps in 4.8 s`. Tests: `crates/cli/tests/replay.rs` (what the journal keeps) and `replay_saves_what_the_session_did_and_runs_it_again_step_by_step` in `tests/mcp.rs` (save, replay, a miss, a step that changes nothing, `from`).

**Cost.** The `replay` tool adds 435 characters to the tool list (17 tools, 5,405 characters). (By 0.1.6 the list had grown to 18 tools and 6,870 characters while the test allowed 9,999; 0.1.7 drops argument docs that repeated their tool's description, to 6,371, and the test's limit is 6,600 again, #34.) A session's journal holds at most 500 steps in memory. An action pays one target lookup on the screen already in memory.

**Limits.** A replay is as deterministic as the app: a dialog that shows on one run in three is a miss on that run, and the model takes that step. No model was in the five replays, and none was measured against them, so the saving in time and tokens is not shown. The journal has every action that worked, including an agent's detours; nothing trims them. Steps saved by position hold only while the layout does, and `save` says how many there are. Typed text that was not marked secret is in the file. A step that needs approval (a payment, a deletion) stops the replay unless the caller passes the approval a typed command would. The nine bench jobs were not replayed ten times each, which is the test the research names.

**Reopen if.** Replays of the bench jobs miss more than one run in twenty (then each step needs a wait for what it produced); people ask for files they can write by hand; a second app's flow shows targets that text and role cannot single out.

**Amended 2026-11-19 (#13 and #15, item 5).** A `shell` step expects exit 0 on replay; a step that timed out or exited otherwise stops the replay. `save` keeps `"expect_exit": n` for a command that exited `n` (not 0) when the run was made, so replaying the same run passes; a hand-written step can set a number, or `null` for any exit. `save` reads the journal without emptying it (`journal {keep: true}`), writes a temporary file beside the target and renames it, then removes the steps it wrote (`journal {drop: n}`), so a failed write loses nothing. A `record start` that cannot clear its steps file stops the recording it began.

**Amended 2026-11-22 (#41, giulioc84's replay benchmark).** A replay of a contact flow saved `+10655555552` for `0655555552` and printed `replayed 6 steps` (#42 fixed the verdict and the phone rule). What changed after it: a typed step whose text is not confirmed stops the replay, including a verdict nothing on screen can give; a saved `type --submit` is sent as the typing, then `press enter` once the field shows the text, since a field that closes on Enter leaves nothing to confirm (one settle more, about 150 ms, and no model call); the first line counts the typed texts verified. A text field the last screen dropped is saved by the label it had, which a replay brings back as a live `type` does (it had been saved as the session's ref). A `scroll --until` that fails after moving the list is saved as the plain scrolls that moved it, without the two at the end that moved nothing, and an action after a failed one is saved without `changed`, which was measured against a screen that may not have been the device's. `save` names each typed value no later `wait` reads back, and `wait` (and `scroll --until`) find a phone number of six or more digits however the app formats it, by the rule a typed field uses, so a read-back step can catch a wrong save. Rejected: failing a replayed `type --submit` whose verdict is unknown, which would stop every saved search; refusing `type --submit` in a file. Tests: `crates/cli/tests/replay.rs`, `save_names_each_typed_value_no_later_wait_reads_back`, `a_wait_for_a_typed_phone_number_finds_it_formatted_but_not_behind_a_country_code`.

## D61. A Windows host reads pixels with the OCR engine Windows ships; Linux has none; OmniParser is not built in

**Choice.** On Windows, `crates/cli/src/ocr.rs` recognizes text with `Windows.Media.Ocr` (the `windows` crate, a Windows-only dependency). It runs on the machine, needs no install and sends nothing anywhere (D9). Everything above it is what macOS already had: a screen with no tree gets `(ocr)` lines in a plain snapshot, `snapshot --pixels` forces them, and they can be tapped by text. The screenshot is enlarged twice before recognition. Linux still answers `unsupported`. Icon detection (OmniParser) is not built in.

**Why.** Screens with no tree (Flutter, WebView, React Native) rank fourth in what users of these tools report, and the one person who has filed an issue here runs Windows (the roadmap research, 2026-11-15). Before this, such a screen on a Windows host gave an empty snapshot and `--pixels` was refused.

**Rejected.** The `ocrs` crate, which the architecture named: it fetches its models from a server at first use, which is a download this project would have to host or ask the user about, and Windows needs neither. Tesseract through its command line: the user must install it, and nothing here could be tried on Linux today. Enlarging three times: 22 strings read against 25, at twice the time.

**OmniParser.** Checked on 2026-11-15 against the model card and the repository: the icon detector (`icon_detect`, finetuned from YOLOv8) is AGPL-3.0, the caption model (`icon_caption`, Florence-2) is MIT. phonedriver is AGPL-3.0-only (D13), so the license does not stand in the way. What does: two neural models that need a runtime this binary does not have, and weights that would be downloaded at first use. Not built; it needs the user's word on the download and a measured case where OCR text is not enough (a game, an icon-only toolbar).

**Evidence.** win-pc (Windows, the emulator's host), 2026-11-15, debug build made there. `cargo test --locked -p phonedriver --test ocr`: `6 passed`: the Android Settings screenshot (17 strings, all read), the iOS testbed screenshot, box positions within 8 points, `p50 37.3 ms, min 36.1, max 39.8, 30 runs`, and the taps tab (its five words and at least 15 of its 24 two-character labels, at one and at two pixels per point). `cargo clippy --locked -p phonedriver --all-targets -- -D warnings` is clean there. End to end from the Windows host against the API 37 emulator, the testbed's `webopaque` tab (a web view with accessibility off): `phonedriver snapshot` printed `@e27 webview` and `@e33 text "Web form" (ocr)` to `@e37 text "Send" (ocr)`; `phonedriver tap Send` printed `ok tapped @e37 "Send" at 31,259`; the testbed's own log then held `{"ev":"web","field":"send"}`. That is the pass test the research set for this item.

**Limits.** Windows reads only the languages installed for the account, and answers `unsupported` with that reason when none has OCR. Short codes are misread (`b0` as `bO`, `b10` as `blO`, `b1` as `bl`). The existing device test for this (`tests/pixels.rs`) still runs on macOS only, because its rig builds the testbed with a Unix script; the Windows run above was done by hand with the same steps. In that run the tab bar's five buttons appear twice, once from the tree and once from pixels: the merge drops a box only when it overlaps a tree element by more than half, and a word's box is much smaller than its button. macOS does the same. No Linux engine. No Windows on ARM run.

**Reopen if.** Someone on Linux asks (then Tesseract's command line, tried on a real Linux host); the duplicate lines cost an agent a wrong tap; a user wants icons found on a screen with no text.

## D62. A screen with nothing to act on arrives with its picture

**Choice.** When a reply's screen has a hollow tree (no elements, or a web view with none inside) and no text read from its pixels, the observation says `empty: true`. The command line adds one hint line: `this screen gives no element list; \`phonedriver screenshot\` shows it, and \`tap x,y\` taps a point on the picture`. The MCP server replaces that line with the picture itself, as an image block in the same tool result, for `snapshot`, every action, `wait` and `batch`.

**Why.** On such a screen the element list gives an agent nothing, and the only way on is the picture and points on it. Without this, every step on a game or a canvas costs two calls and two model turns: the action, then `screenshot`. On a Linux host, which has no text recognition (D61), every web view with accessibility off is such a screen.

**Rejected.** A picture with every reply: a 411 by 923 PNG is about 27 kB and some 500 tokens, paid on screens whose element list already says everything. Calling a screen sparse by how much of it its elements cover: a home screen is mostly wallpaper and is not sparse; the rule that held is the one the daemon already used to decide when to read pixels (`tree_is_hollow`).

**Evidence.** `a_screen_with_nothing_to_act_on_comes_with_its_picture` and `a_screen_with_elements_comes_without_a_picture` in `crates/cli/tests/mcp.rs` (a `SurfaceView` and nothing else: the snapshot and a tap at a point each return one image block and the note; a screen with a button returns none). Live on the API 37 emulator through `phonedriver mcp` on the Mac mini, the testbed's `webopaque` tab, in a call where Apple Vision failed: the `snapshot` result was the tab bar's lines, `@e7 webview`, the note, and `image/png` of 26,780 bytes, which I opened: the web form with its Send button at 31,259. In a later call Vision worked, the reply had ten `(ocr)` lines and no picture, and `tap` at `30,259` made the testbed log `{"ev":"web","field":"send"}`.

**Limits.** A screen with one stray label is not empty and gets no picture; `screenshot` is still there for it. The picture is one pixel per point. No agent run measures the calls saved. Not tried on a game.

**Reopen if.** An agent run on a canvas app shows it still asks for screenshots (then the rule is too narrow); the pictures show up in a token audit of ordinary runs (then it is too wide).

## D63. `press fingerprint` touches an Android emulator's fingerprint sensor

**Choice.** `fingerprint` and `wrong-fingerprint` are keys of `press` (CLI, MCP and `batch`). On an Android emulator they touch the emulated sensor with finger 1 or finger 2 through the emulator's console (`crates/android/src/console.rs`: the port in the serial, `auth` with the token from `.emulator_console_auth_token`, `finger touch N`, `finger remove`). A test enrolls with `press fingerprint`, so finger 1 is the enrolled one and finger 2 never is. These two keys are also the only actions sent to a screen with a secure lock, since unlocking is what they are for. A physical phone answers `unsupported` (its sensor takes its owner's finger), and so does iOS.

**Why.** The user asked for biometric simulation as a first-class tool. An app under test that asks for a fingerprint stops an agent dead: the prompt needs a person (architecture 14). On an emulator the sensor is a console command, which adb's own client reaches with `adb emu`; phonedriver speaks to the adb server itself (D11) and had no way to the console.

**Rejected.** A new verb and MCP tool (`biometric match`): a touch of a sensor is a button press, `press` exists, and the two words cost 69 characters in its description against a tool of its own. The agent's shell on the device: the sensor is not reachable from inside the emulator without root. Reaching the console through a remote adb server: the console listens on the emulator's own machine only, so with `ADB_SERVER_SOCKET` the reply is `unsupported` and says to run phonedriver there. Face ID and Touch ID on the iOS Simulator (`notifyutil` notifications): not built, because nothing here could prove it against an app today.

**Evidence.** win-pc, the daemon running on the emulator's own machine (Windows build), API 37 emulator, 2026-11-15. With a test PIN set by `locksettings set-pin`, fingerprint enrollment opened from Settings: three `phonedriver press fingerprint` calls took the screen from `Touch the sensor` through `progress "0%"` and `progress "50%"` to `Fingerprint added`. Then `press lock`: `dumpsys window policy` said `showing=true` and `dumpsys fingerprint` `"accept":0,"reject":0`. `press wrong-fingerprint` (which woke the screen first): `showing=true`, `"reject":1`. A tap was refused: `The device is locked`. `press fingerprint`: `showing=false`, `"accept":1`. Afterwards the PIN and the fingerprint were removed and the lock screen turned off again, as it had been. Tests: four for the console client against a fake console (the order of what it sends, no token when none is asked for, a refusal, a missing token) and `crates/cli/tests/fingerprint.rs` (the keys reach the device; they are sent to a locked screen that refuses `press home`).

**Limits.** Emulators only, and only when phonedriver runs on the machine that runs the emulator. No app's own fingerprint prompt was tried, only enrollment and the lock screen: the testbed has no such prompt. One API level. No face unlock. Nothing for iOS.

**Reopen if.** Someone tests an app's biometric prompt on the Simulator (then the `notifyutil` path, with a prompt in the iOS testbed to prove it); the emulator stops accepting `finger touch`.

## D64. On macOS a screenshot the Accurate reader refuses is read by the Fast one

**Choice.** `recognize` on macOS asks Vision at the Accurate level (revision 2) and, when that call fails, reads the same picture at the Fast level, enlarged to about three pixels a point through Core Image (`CIImage` with a scale transform, handed to `VNImageRequestHandler`). Once the Accurate level has refused a picture that the Fast level then read, the process stops asking it (`recognize_at` still reads at a named level, for the tests).

**Why.** On macOS 27.0.1 the Accurate level can stop working inside a running process. It compiles a Neural Engine model on its first call (13 s on the Mac mini, 21 to 31 s on the MacBook) and macOS keeps the result in `~/Library/Caches/<program>/com.apple.e5rt.e5bundlecache`. On the Mac mini that folder was empty for every program, Apple's own daemons included (3 GB of disk free; the MacBook, with 9 GB free, writes four files there). With no cache, the model reads until the process has been idle for about 8 s; after that every call fails in 10 ms with `CRImageReaderError 1`, under `e5rt … create_precompiled_compute_operation_with_options call failed (Code: 13)` in the system log. A daemon is idle between calls, so on such a Mac `snapshot --pixels` worked once and never again, and the OCR tests failed in whole runs. The Fast level does not load that model and kept reading.

**Rejected.** Asking the Accurate level on every call, which this decision first chose because a refusal took 10 ms: the same evening the mini's refusals took seconds each (the speed test's 33 reads took 222 s), and no process was ever seen to get the level back. The Fast level always: it reads 28 of the 30 strings of the taps tab against 30. Enlarging in Rust with the `png` crate: 10 million samples a frame in a debug build, against one Core Image call; `objc2-core-image` was already in the lock file under `objc2-vision`. Compute-device settings (`usesCPUOnly`, `setComputeDevice` to CPU or GPU): tried on the mini, no change. Revision 3: fails outright there after 14 s.

**Evidence.** Mac mini, 2026-11-15. A probe that calls `recognize` 40 times on the taps tab with an 8 s pause after call 20, run three times with the same result: calls 1 to 20 read at the Accurate level (the first in 13.4 to 13.6 s, the rest in 35 to 73 ms), calls 21 to 40 came from the Fast level in 16 to 63 ms with 30 boxes each, and no call failed. Before the change the same idle made `Apple Vision could not read this screenshot` the reply. `--test ocr`: 8 passed, among them `the_quick_reader_reads_the_same_pictures_when_the_accurate_one_cannot` (the taps tab at one and two pixels a point, at least 19 of its 24 labels exact; Settings; the iOS testbed; a box in the right place). Fast level measured through Core Image on the taps tab at one pixel a point: 20 strings exact as it is, 26 enlarged twice, 28 enlarged three times.

**Limits.** A daemon that met one refusal reads at the Fast level until it exits, also if macOS would have recovered. The cause in macOS is not found: an empty cache and a nearly full disk were seen together on one Mac, and that is all. The first read in a new process still takes 13 s there, every time. The Fast level misreads more (`Busy` and `b0` on the taps tab). The reply does not say which level read the screen.

**Reopen if.** A later macOS writes the cache on that Mac (then check whether the fallback still runs at all); the Fast level's misreads show up in agent runs.

**Superseded by D88** (2026-11-16): Vision reads at the Fast level only, so no process waits for the Accurate level's model.

## D65. A Linux host reads pixels with Tesseract when it is installed, after cleaning the picture for it

**Choice.** On Linux (every host that is neither macOS nor Windows), `recognize` runs the `tesseract` program found on the `PATH`: `tesseract stdin stdout --psm 11 tsv`, the picture on its standard input, its word table parsed by `phonedriver_core::ocr::from_tesseract_tsv`. Nothing is linked and nothing is downloaded; without the program the reply is `unsupported` and names the package to install (`tesseract-ocr`). Before the picture is handed over it is made grey, enlarged to three pixels a point, and each pixel is replaced by how far it is from the mean of the square of 16 points around it (24 levels or fewer are white, three times the rest is the darkness). Words Tesseract is less than 60% sure of are left out. This replaces the "Linux has none" of D61.

**Why.** The user asked for the Linux code and named the Ubuntu on win-pc to try it on. D61's "reopen if" named this path. Tesseract is the one engine a Linux user can get from their distribution with one command, it is Apache-2.0, and running the program keeps the release binary free of a library that may not be there. The cleaning is not optional: Tesseract's page analysis takes a grid of grey buttons for pictures. Measured on the testbed's taps tab (30 strings) with Tesseract 4.1.1, as the picture comes: 1 read at page modes 3 and 6, 2 at modes 11 and 12, the same enlarged two, three and four times, and none with the colours inverted (a dark theme). After the cleaning: 29 of 30 light and 29 of 30 dark; a radius of 8 points read 26 to 28, a radius of 16 without the 24-level floor read 29 but once took 27 s on the dark picture.

**Rejected.** Linking `libtesseract` through a crate: a build-time dependency on development packages and a release binary that fails to start where the library is missing. The `ocrs` crate: it downloads its models (D61). Bundling Tesseract or its language data: tens of megabytes for a feature most Linux hosts with a tree never use. A fixed black-and-white threshold: it has to know whether the theme is dark. Keeping every word Tesseract returns: icons come back as words (`eee` at 26 to 53, a quote mark at 49); every word that was on the two screens came back at 84 or more.

**Evidence.** Ubuntu 22.04 under WSL2 on win-pc, 2026-11-15, Tesseract 4.1.1 unpacked from the distribution's packages into a home folder (no root was used). `cargo test --locked -p phonedriver --test ocr`: 5 passed, among them `tesseract_reads_the_taps_tab_light_and_dark_at_one_and_two_pixels_per_point` (the six words and at least 21 of the 24 labels, four pictures), Settings (17 strings), the iOS testbed, and a box in the right place. The same tests with no `tesseract` on the `PATH`: 5 passed in 0.75 s, each after checking that the reply names `tesseract-ocr`. `from_tesseract_tsv` has a unit test in `phonedriver-core`, which runs on every host. Clippy clean on Linux. A release build took 325 to 411 ms a frame on the taps tab and 443 to 488 ms on Settings.

**Limits.** Not run against a device from a Linux host: this WSL has no way to the adb server on its Windows side (NAT networking), so `snapshot --pixels` end to end rests on the Mac and Windows runs of the same code above `recognize`. English only, the language Tesseract loads by default. `b0` reads as `bo`. Eight times slower than Vision. Tesseract 4.1.1 only; version 5 was not tried. CI has no Tesseract, so there it tests the reply that names the package and nothing else.

**Reopen if.** A Linux user reports misreads on a real app (then try Tesseract 5 and a language option); the 340 ms is felt in agent runs; a distribution ships a Tesseract whose TSV differs.

## D66. The iOS Simulator is recorded by its own recorder

**Choice.** `record start` on a Simulator runs `xcrun simctl io <udid> recordVideo --codec=h264 --force <path>` and returns when that program prints `Recording started`. `record stop` interrupts it (SIGINT, through `kill -INT`), waits up to 30 s for it to finish its file, and reads the file's size, frame count and length with `phonedriver_video::record::probe`. Dropping the device interrupts a recorder still running. The reply, the steps file and the MCP form are those of D59. A physical iPhone answers `unsupported`.

**Why.** The user asked for iOS recording. The Simulator tier has no video stream of ours: it polls small screenshots from the runner, twice a second when idle, so there is nothing to mux as D59 does on Android. The Simulator's recorder is on every Mac with Xcode, writes H.264 at the screen's full size (1206x2622 for an iPhone 17), and needs no code on the device.

**Rejected.** Building a video from the runner's screenshots: a few frames a second at best, and each one costs a request the agent's own calls wait behind. Killing the recorder at the stop: it writes the file's index when interrupted, and a killed one leaves a file that does not open. Leaving its output unread after the start line: it prints two more lines at the stop and would be stopped by the closed pipe before the index is written, so a task reads them to the end. HEVC, the recorder's default: H.264 is what the Android files are, and what every player opens.

**Evidence.** Mac mini, iPhone 17 Simulator, iOS 26.5, Xcode 27, 2026-11-15. By hand first: the start line came about 300 ms after the program was run; an interrupt ended it with exit 0 and `Wrote video to:`; 4.4 s of Safari opening was 4.4 MB, 249 frames. Live test `a_recording_of_the_simulator_is_an_mp4_of_the_taps_made_while_it_ran` (`--ignored`, `device_live`): passed in 17 s; its file: H.264, 1206x2622, 98 frames, 2.96 s, three `tap` events in the testbed's log. Through the command line: `record start --output`, a tap on B17, the Text tab, 23 characters typed, the List tab, a scroll, `record stop`: `5.6 s, 1206x2622, 207 frames, 1.1 MB` and `steps: 5 with their times`; a sheet of two frames a second shows `Last tap: none`, then `Last tap: b17`, the empty Text tab, the typed text over the keyboard, the list, the list scrolled to Row 8, each at the time its step line gives. `probe` is tested on a 9 kB file the recorder wrote of a still screen (`moov` before `mdat`, a `wide` box, one frame, a movie 1.3 s long) and on a file `Recording` wrote.

**Limits.** Physical iPhones are not recorded: the CoreDevice tier decodes an H.264 stream that D59's muxer could take, and no iPhone was at hand to prove it. The recorder writes a frame only when the screen changes, so the last frame can sit well before the stop (4.85 s in a 5.6 s movie above); players hold it. No sound. A daemon that is killed leaves the recorder running until the Simulator shuts down. One Simulator, one iOS version.

**Reopen if.** An iPhone is available for a session (then tap the CoreDevice stream as D59 does); `simctl` changes the line it prints at the start.

## D67. A saved step finds its element by id first, with its text beside it

**Choice.** When `save` replaces a ref (D60), it keeps, in this order: the element's id, if the app gave it one and no other element on the screen the agent saw shares it, together with the element's label or hint and its role (`{"id":…,"text":…,"role":…}`); the label or a hint with the role, when those single it out; the element's centre. The new target form `{id, text?, role?}` is resolved by id, and by the text when no element or more than one has the id. Ids that begin `android:id/` are never kept. The command line has no syntax for an id target; saved files are its only writer.

**Why.** D60 said to reopen when a second app's flow showed targets that text and role cannot single out. The replay evaluation showed three in eleven tasks (`docs/bench/replay.md`): the Files app's folder-name field, the testbed's web form fields and a Compose switch were kept as positions, and the Files dialog was 156 points higher in the replay than when it was saved. The tool the user pointed at for comparison, agent-device's replay scripts, keeps `id="…" || label="…"` for the same reason. An id survives a label that changes (a count in it, a new wording, another language); the text beside it survives a build that renames ids. `android:id/button1` is the confirming button of every dialog, so a step kept by it would press whatever dialog is up.

**Rejected.** Text first and the id as the fallback: the label is what changes between runs of an app under test, and a changed label would send every such step to the model. The id alone: one renamed id would end the replay. A role alone ("the only text field"): not needed once ids and hints are used, and it says nothing about which field was meant. Waiting for each step's target before failing, variables for typed text, and a digest of the file for resuming, all of which agent-device has: no replay in the evaluation failed for lack of them, so they are not built. Trimming an agent's detours from the journal: nothing shows which steps mattered.

**Evidence.** `an_element_is_saved_by_an_id_of_its_own_with_its_text_beside_it` in `crates/cli/tests/replay.rs`, red first (`left: {"point":{"x":180.0,"y":130.0}}, right: {"id":"name"}`): an id of its own, an id with a hint, an id two fields share (kept by position), a platform id (kept by position), a hint alone; then an `act` by id, by a renamed id with its text, by a shared id (`ambiguous`) and by an id nothing has (`not found`). Live: five tasks on the API 37 emulator, ten replays each, 50 of 50 passed their device checks, with 16 targets kept by id and none by position; `testbed-web-form`, 0 of 10 on the build before, was 10 of 10. The whole table is `docs/bench/replay.md`.

**Limits.** One emulator, Android only: no iOS replay was run, where the id is the accessibility identifier. No app was replayed after its labels or ids changed, so "survives a renamed label" rests on the unit test. An id that is unique when saved and shared when replayed falls to the text; with no text it is `ambiguous` and the step goes to the model.

**Reopen if.** A replay presses the wrong thing through an id (then keep the role with it as a check); iOS replays show identifiers that repeat.

## D68. The emulator's console is reached through the emulator when adb is on another machine

**Choice.** `press fingerprint` and `press wrong-fingerprint` no longer refuse when adb is behind `ADB_SERVER_SOCKET`. The console listens on the loopback of the machine that runs the emulator; inside the emulator that loopback is `10.0.2.2`. So the daemon opens `exec:nc 10.0.2.2 <port>` on the device through adb and speaks the console's lines over that stream (`console::touch_sensor_on`). The token is still read from `.emulator_console_auth_token` in the home folder here, so for a remote emulator the user copies that one file over once. With adb on this machine the console is reached directly, as in D63.

**Why.** The user asked for the fingerprint key to work beyond D63's two limits: only on the emulator's own machine, and never tried on an app's prompt. The first is this project's own test setup (the daemon on a Mac, the emulators on a PC) and any farm of emulators. The second needed a prompt to try it on, which the Android testbed now has.

**Rejected.** adb's `tcp:<port>:<host>` service: adbd refuses connections to other hosts. `adb emu`: it is the adb client that connects to the console, on its own machine, and phonedriver speaks to the adb server itself (D11). A variable or a flag for the token: a file with the name the emulator already uses needs no new setting, and the token never passes through a command line. Sending the lines as a shell command (`printf … | nc`): the token would be in the device's process list for a moment; over `exec:` it is only bytes on the stream.

**Evidence.** Mac mini, adb through the tunnel to win-pc, API 37 emulator, 2026-11-15, the token file in a temporary home that was deleted afterwards. Enrollment from Settings: three `press fingerprint` calls took `Touch the sensor` to `progress "0%"`, `"49%"`, `"100%"` and `Fingerprint added`. Then the testbed's new `biometric` screen, which shows the system's `BiometricPrompt` as an app does before a payment: `tap "Confirm with fingerprint"` showed `heading "Confirm payment"`, `Pad thai, $12.00` and `image "Fingerprint sensor"`; `press wrong-fingerprint` changed it to `Try again` with `Not recognized`, and the app logged `{"ev":"biometric","result":"failed"}`; `press fingerprint` closed the prompt, the app logged `{"ev":"biometric","result":"succeeded"}` and the screen said `Payment confirmed`. Unit test `a_touch_goes_over_a_connection_that_is_already_open` (the same three lines over an in-memory stream). Afterwards the PIN and the fingerprint were removed and the lock screen turned off again (`get-disabled` `true`, no `fingerId`).

**Limits.** One emulator, one API level. `nc` has to be on the emulator image (it is on API 37; older images were not tried). An emulator with custom networking may not have `10.0.2.2`. The remote path has no automated test against a device; the local path was not run again after the code was shared between the two. Face unlock is not built. iOS is D69.

**Reopen if.** An image without `nc` is used (then a forwarder in the on-device agent); someone needs the remote path without copying the file.

## D69. On an iOS Simulator the fingerprint keys answer Face ID

**Choice.** On a Simulator, `press fingerprint` posts the notification the Simulator's Features menu posts for a matching face, `com.apple.BiometricKit_Sim.pearl.match`, and the Touch ID one, `…fingerTouch.match`; `press wrong-fingerprint` posts the two `nomatch` ones. Both go through `xcrun simctl spawn <udid> notifyutil`. Before either, a Simulator whose `com.apple.BiometricKit.enrollmentChanged` state is not 1 is enrolled (the state set to 1 and the notification posted). A physical iPhone answers `unsupported`. The keys keep their Android names.

**Why.** The user asked for the fingerprint work to reach iOS; D63 left it out because nothing here could prove it. The iOS testbed now has a button that asks `LAContext` for Face ID, so it can be proven. The enrolling is in the key because an app asks `canEvaluatePolicy` before it shows anything: on a fresh Simulator the testbed logged `{"result":"unavailable","code":-7}` (not enrolled) and no prompt came up, so there was nothing for a match to answer.

**Rejected.** New key names (`face`, `wrong-face`): two more words in the `press` description for the same act, and an agent that learned the Android pair would not find them. A separate enroll command: one more thing to know, for a state a Simulator can always be put in. Leaving it to `shell` (`notifyutil` runs through it already): the notification names are not something an agent should have to remember. Posting only the Face ID notification: a Simulator with Touch ID would answer `ok` and do nothing.

**Evidence.** Mac mini, iPhone 17 Simulator, iOS 26.5, 2026-11-15. By hand: after enrolling, `tap "Confirm with Face ID"` brought up the system's prompt; `pearl.nomatch` gave `alert "Face Not Recognised"` with `Try Face ID Again` and `Cancel`; `pearl.match` closed it, the testbed logged `{"code":0,"ev":"biometric","result":"succeeded"}` and showed `Payment confirmed`. Live test `a_face_id_prompt_is_failed_by_the_wrong_face_and_passed_by_the_right_one` (`--ignored`, `device_live`), from an unenrolled Simulator: the first ask is `unavailable`; after `Button::Fingerprint` the ask shows the prompt; `Button::WrongFingerprint` puts `Not Recogni…` in the tree and the app has no answer yet; `Button::Fingerprint` makes the app log `succeeded`. `1 passed … finished in 12.31s`. The test leaves the Simulator unenrolled, as it found it.

**Limits.** Face ID only was run: no Simulator with Touch ID (an iPhone SE) was at hand, so the `fingerTouch` notifications are sent untried. One iOS version. The key enrolls a Simulator without saying so in its reply. The lock screen was not tried. Real iPhones never.

**Reopen if.** A Touch ID Simulator shows the `fingerTouch` names are wrong; Apple renames the notifications in a later Xcode.

## D70. A secret is masked wherever it comes back, for as long as the daemon runs

**Choice.** The daemon remembers every text it is given with `secret: true` (the text of a `type`, alone or as a batch step, and the command of a `shell`) and replaces each with `•••` in every string of every reply and error it returns from then on, to every session, before the reply is traced (`daemon::secrets`, called once in `Daemon::call`). The memory is a list in the daemon's process, never written, gone when the daemon stops. A secret of fewer than four characters is masked only in the reply to the call that carried it. A screenshot's `data_b64` is skipped. The mask that was threaded through `finish` and `report` for one `type` reply, and the one in `shell`, are gone. Beside it: after an action fails, a `type` with no target reads the screen again before trusting which field is focused (`Session::unsure`), and `FakeDevice`'s call log holds a `ShellRequest`, whose `Debug` prints a length.

**Why.** AGENTS.md promises that text passed with `--secret` never reaches logs, traces, errors or test output, and the reply already masked it, so the promise includes what the model reads. A property test of the real daemon against a fake phone with a note field and a password field (`crates/cli/tests/secret_property.rs`, 3,000 generated sessions) found four ways it broke:

- F1. A secret typed into a field that shows its text (a username, an API key in a settings form, a card number) was masked in that one reply and then printed by the next `snapshot`, `find`, or any action's reply, and written to the trace file under `--trace-full`. 108 of 3,000 sessions leaked through a snapshot reply and 217 wrote a trace file holding one.
- F2. A `type` with `secret` as a `batch` step was not masked at all: `[Type { flagged: true, into: Note, in_batch: true }]` returned `~ @e1 textbox "Note" "Ty0mW4zR8nB3c" focused`. The CLI and MCP refuse a secret in a batch, so only another client of the socket could reach it.
- F3. A secret `type` into the password field tapped it, then the device failed the text. The session still held the screen from before the tap, so the next plain `type` with no target believed the note field was focused and pasted plain text into the password field, past D31's refusal. 1 of 3,000 sessions.
- F4. `FakeDevice` logged a shell command as a `String`, so a failed assertion that printed the call log printed a `shell --secret` command.

One mask at the one place every reply leaves through covers a reply shape nobody has written yet; the per-call mask covered one.

**Rejected.** Masking element values when the screen is rendered: it misses errors, shell output and `logs`, and needs a call in every path that builds a screen. Remembering per session: a second session on the same phone (a person's terminal with `--share`) would be shown what the first typed. Remembering short secrets too: `12` would blank every label and count holding those digits, for every later call; four characters is where a PIN starts. Writing the list to disk so a restarted daemon still masks: that is a file of secrets. Refusing `secret` in a batch at the daemon: it is masked now, and the refusal already lives where the text is read.

**Evidence.** Mac mini, 2026-11-15. Before: the property failed on its first case (F2), and the survey printed 18 kinds of leak. After: `cargo test --locked -p phonedriver --test secret_property -- --include-ignored`: `0 kinds of leak`, `4 passed`. Each of ten breaks of the product code makes the check fail (`.claude/skills/verify-secrets/SKILL.md` has the table).

**Limits.** A fake phone with two fields; no real device. Pixels: a screenshot, a recording and a `--trace-screens` copy show what the phone shows. A secret under four characters shows on later screens. A device that prints a secret back changed (escaped, split over lines, cut short) is not matched; since D105 the escaped forms, spaced-out forms, a cut start and a field's reformatted value are. Text typed without `secret` is not a secret to the daemon, whatever it is. A label that happens to hold a remembered secret's characters is masked too. A daemon that restarts forgets, so a full trace written after a restart can hold a secret still on the screen.

**Added the same day (F5).** A review found one place the daemon writes text to disk itself: the steps file of a recording (`Taping::step`) holds each action's summary, and a tap on a label that shows a remembered secret wrote `tapped @e4 "<the secret>"` there. The actor now holds the same list (`Secrets::masked`) and masks the summary before it is written. The property test taps such a label while recording, and break B11 shows it failing.

**Reopen if.** A real secret shows up in a reply in a changed form; masking a remembered secret hides something an agent needed to read.


**Update 2026-11-22 (audit).** An argument the MCP server cannot read is reported without the value serde quotes (`invalid type: string "…", expected a boolean` becomes `a field has the wrong type or value, expected a boolean`), on both protocols and in batch steps: a secret given in the wrong field (`"secret": "<the password>"`) was echoed back in the error.
## D71. A web page is read as text through the browser's own debugging socket

**Choice.** `page` (daemon method; `phonedriver page [--grep words] [--all]`; in MCP the `page` argument of `snapshot`, with `find` for the words) returns the title, the address and the text of the web page the app in front shows, in one call. Android only. The host looks in `/proc/net/unix` for DevTools sockets: `webview_devtools_remote_<pid>` when the pid is the front app's own, otherwise a browser's (`chrome_devtools_remote`) whose `/json/version` names the front app in `Android-Package`. It asks `/json` for the pages, takes the one that is shown (a web view says `visible` in its description; a Chrome tab is asked `document.visibilityState`, at most 12 of them), and evaluates one fixed expression over the WebSocket: the title, `location.href` and `document.body.innerText`. Everything goes through the adb connection the daemon already holds, to the abstract socket (`crates/android/src/devtools.rs`, 250 lines without its tests; no new dependency, no port forward, nothing installed on the phone). The daemon drops blank lines, cuts at a line end before 8,000 characters and says how many lines and characters are left; `grep` keeps the lines with one of the words and the two lines after each. It only reads: no click, no typing and no navigation goes through this door.

**Why.** A web page is the worst case for the snapshot loop. An article is many screens, each one a scroll and a tree, and the accessibility tree of a web view is where roles and labels are thinnest. Chrome, and any web view of a debug build, already serve the page's text to whoever may open their debugging socket, and the shell user may. The roadmap research of 2026-11-15 listed a DOM bridge and set its test: fewer tokens and no lost passes on our web jobs. This one reads through the socket and keeps acting on the screen, where the approval guard (D31) sees the element that is tapped and the action is checked against the next screen.

**Rejected.** Acting through DevTools (`Runtime.evaluate` with a click, `Input.dispatchMouseEvent`, `Page.navigate`): the guard reads what is tapped from the screen's tree, and a script's click would pass it unseen and unverified. Returning the DOM, or DevTools' accessibility tree: several times the text, and the screen's tree already serves acting. `adb forward` to a TCP port: it opens the page's debugger to every process on the host for as long as it stands; the abstract socket through our own adb client opens nothing. A WebSocket crate: one request and one reply need a handshake, a masked frame out and frames in, about 60 lines. Turning web debugging on in other apps: it needs root or a rebuilt app, and it is not ours to change. An `all` argument in MCP: the tool list is read on every turn, and `find` narrows a long page. Asking every DevTools socket: Android freezes an app in the background, and a connection to a frozen Chrome's socket is accepted and never answered, so the front app's own socket is tried first and a browser is given 1.5 s.

**Evidence.** API 37 emulator through the win-pc tunnel, daemon on the Mac mini (debug build). 2026-11-15, Chrome on mobile Wikipedia (Ada Lovelace): `page` answered in 0.4 to 0.6 s with the page whole, 83 lines and 6,182 characters; `page --grep "born|died"` gave the two labels with the dates under them. The testbed's web view (a debug build) answered in 0.5 s. Settings answered `com.android.settings has no web page that can be read`. Before the front app's own socket was tried first, the same read timed out on the testbed because a frozen Chrome was asked first.

2026-11-16, one long page, `https://www.gnu.org/licenses/gpl-3.0.html` in Chrome, read to its end both ways from the command line (`pd-page-measure.sh` on the mini):

| | calls | time | characters returned |
|---|---|---|---|
| `page --all` | 1 | 0.54 s | 35,738 (159 lines) |
| `snapshot`, then `scroll down` until nothing moves | 46 | 94.8 s | 40,895 |
| `page` (the default cut) | 1 | 0.49 s | 8,280, ending `… 113 more lines (27460 characters)` |

So the gain on a long page is calls and time, 46 to 1 and 95 s to half a second; the text itself is 13% smaller, not several times. An agent pays for every call again in the context it re-reads, which this table does not count.

The agent runs do not show a saving, and the roadmap's test asked for one. `bench/agent-eval`, `claude -p` on the subscription, the new build: wiki-birthdate passed 3 of 3 at 28,758, 36,393 and 20,651 tokens (4, 4 and 2 calls); chrome-heading passed 2 of 2 at 35,208 and 27,986. The agents used `page` in 3 of the 5 runs. The one valid run of the old build that day: wiki-birthdate, 28,182 tokens, 3 calls. Both answers are on the first screen, so there was nothing to scroll for; no pass was lost. The emulator died during the old build's other runs, so there is no second number to set beside these. No job in the set needs the end of a long page, and until one does the token claim is unproven.

Tests: `crates/android/src/devtools.rs` has four (socket names, the WebSocket frames against a fake server, which target is shown); `--test daemon` has three for the cut, the one-line page and `grep`; `--test mcp` has one for `snapshot` with `page`; the secrets property reads a page that prints every earlier secret back.

**Limits.** Android only; iOS has nothing equivalent wired (Safari's Web Inspector speaks another protocol and was not tried). Chrome, and web views of apps that are debug builds or call `setWebContentsDebuggingEnabled`; every other app, a banking app's web view among them, answers that it has no page that can be read. Other Chromium browsers were not tried: one that names its socket differently is not found. `innerText` is what the page renders: a section the page keeps closed (mobile Wikipedia closes all but the first) is not in it until it is opened on the screen; text in a canvas, an image or a cross-origin frame is not in it; form values are not in it. The text carries no refs: to act on something the page mentions, find it with a plain `snapshot`. A page of one very long line is cut by characters. A page that is still loading gives what has loaded. A secret typed earlier and shown on the page is masked like any other reply (D70); a password already on the page is not something the daemon knows. One emulator, one Chrome version, one debug-build app (the testbed).

**Reopen if.** Agents need to act on what they read without a second look at the screen (then refs must tie the page's text to the tree); a common browser is not found by its socket name; someone asks for iOS.

## D72. Several agents on one phone, each on its own hidden display: spiked, and not built

**Superseded by D73** (2026-11-16, the same day): it is built. The spike's findings and the one-task limit below stand.

**Choice.** A session keeps the whole device, as before: one lease for a device, one hidden display at most (D32). Several sessions on one phone at once, each on a virtual display of its own, is not built. Several devices at once already work, one actor for each.

**Why.** The roadmap listed it and the user asked for it, so it was spiked first (`docs/spikes/lanes.md`, API 37 emulator, 2026-11-16). The mechanics hold: one shell process opened two virtual displays with an encoder each, read both trees (11 ms for both at once), and in ten rounds of a tap on each display in the same instant neither took the other's input; keys followed the display they were sent to, each display kept its own focus and keyboard, and every window event carried its display's id. What does not hold is the use. An ordinary app is one task: launching Settings or our testbed on the second display, with or without `FLAG_ACTIVITY_MULTIPLE_TASK`, moved it off the first. Only an app that supports several windows (Chrome) ran on both. So the case that would pay, several flows of the one app under test on one emulator, cannot be served, and the case that can be served, different apps side by side, has no one asking for it. The cost is high and lands in the wrong place: the agent starts with its display, owns the one `UiAutomation` connection, and is killed and restarted when the mode changes; sharing it between sessions means rewriting how it starts, attaches and stops, for every user of the default path.

**Rejected.** Building it for the different-apps case: a rewrite of the start path for a use nobody has. Running each flow as another Android user or a work profile, which would give an app a second instance: not tried; both change how the phone is set up, which is not something to do to a person's phone, and Android documents a visible background user for cars only. A second on-device agent process for each lane: the system registers one `UiAutomation` at a time, which is why two daemons on one device stop each other's agents today.

**Evidence.** `docs/spikes/lanes.md` has the table. The spike's code is deleted.

**Limits.** One emulator, software encoder, two displays, the shell user. No physical phone. Whether a hardware encoder allows several sessions was not tried.

**Reopen if.** Someone needs two different apps driven at once on one phone and a second device will not do; Android lets an ordinary app run a second instance on another display; a CI user would rather wait for lanes than start a second emulator.

## D73. Several sessions on one Android phone, each on a lane: a hidden display of its own

**Choice.** A device id may end in `+<name>` (`android:emulator-5554+a`; `+a` alone when one device is connected; the name is 1 to 8 lowercase letters or digits). Such an id is a lane: its own actor, lease, refs and trace in the daemon, and on the phone a hidden display of its own (Android 13 and later, the display of D32). The phone's own screen (the plain id) and the unnamed hidden display (`--display hidden`) work as before, beside the lanes. On the device there is still one agent process, because the system registers one `UiAutomation`: every lane's control and video connection goes to it, and a connection says in its first frame which screen it is for (`"display":"hidden","lane":"a"`). The agent opens the display and its encoder then, not at process start, and a thread belongs to the lane of the thread that started it (`Lane.java`), so the input, windows, launches, focus and video of a connection stay on its display. Window events go to the lane whose display they name (`AccessibilityEvent.getDisplayId()`, API 34; before it every lane hears every event). The host keeps one process handle for an adb server and serial, shared by the lanes (`agent.rs`, `running`); the process ends when its last connection closes. One paste runs at a time, because the clipboard is one for the phone. `devices` lists each open lane as a row. On a hidden display the app in front is the display's topmost application window, and a launch there waits while nothing is in front.

**Why.** D72 declined this on the same day: the use looked narrow and the change is in the start path. The user answered that risk and experiments are welcome, which is theirs to decide. What it gives: several agents, or one agent's helpers, on one emulator at the same time, each with its own lease, and with no new tool, flag or argument, because every MCP tool and CLI verb already takes a device. The risk is held in how it is built: the phone's own screen sends the same hello as before, the tests for the hidden display and the daemon pass unchanged, and the lane paths have their own tests.

**Rejected.** A `lane` argument on every tool: seventeen tool schemas are read on every turn and each would grow, while the device id already goes everywhere. A process for each lane: one `UiAutomation`. Opening the display at process start from an argument, as D32 did: a second lane cannot restart the process the first one is using. A second user or a work profile, which would give one app a second instance: it changes how the phone is set up; not tried. Slow-link video settings for a hidden display: its hello includes opening the display, so its time says nothing about the link.

**Evidence.** API 37 emulator on win-pc (real GPU), daemon on the Mac mini through an ssh tunnel, 2026-11-16, `~/pd-lanes.sh`, at a time when the PC's disk was quiet.

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Lane `a` launches the testbed and lane `b` launches Settings at the same moment, from a daemon that had neither open | 3.4 s and 5.0 s for both (two runs); each reply lists its own app's screen |
| `devices` | the phone's own screen, `+a` held by session `one` on display 49, `+b` held by `two` on display 50 |
| Five rounds: a tap on a testbed button on `a` and on `Network & internet` on `b` at the same moment | the testbed's own log has `b1` to `b5`, one each, at the tapped points; `b` opened the page in every round of the first run and in two of five in the second (see Limits); 2.0 to 2.6 s a round with both settles |
| The phone's own screen during all of it | Clock, opened there before the lanes, still in front |
| Session `two` asks for lane `a` | `device android:emulator-5554+a is leased by session one` |
| Session `one` releases `a` | `a` leaves `devices`; `b` takes the next key |
| Lane `a` launches Settings while it is open on `b` | it moves to `a` in 1.2 to 1.5 s; `b` is on its home screen |
| `--display hidden` and then the plain device, after the lanes | both work as before |

Found by these runs and fixed before the numbers above. The agent decided it was alone when its first hello was still opening a display (the ten-second check ran during it). A lane's hello looked like a slow link, and the slow-link bit rate was kept for the whole process, so the next lane's encoder ran at 400 kbit/s and a screenshot after a tap was a blur of the screen before it. `isActive()` is one flag for the phone, so the lane touched less recently reported no app in front. A launch on a display that had no window yet answered `launched` over an empty screen. Fake-device tests: `--test lanes` (7).

**Limits.** An ordinary app is one task: a launch on a second lane moves it there and leaves the first lane on its home screen. So lanes serve different apps side by side, and apps that open several windows. The first version had an open defect: a screenshot taken right after a tap on a lane showed the screen from before it. Its cause and the fix are D74. One emulator; no physical phone; three lanes or more not tried; API 33, which has no display of its own focus, not tried. A hidden display's stream stays at the agent's own bit rate on a slow link. A lane that reconnects in the instant after it closed can be refused as in use; not seen. The PC's disk was saturated by other work for most of the session, and runs made then are not counted: launches took 8 to 82 s and Chrome stayed on its splash on a lane.

**Reopen if.** Someone needs the same app on two lanes; a phone refuses a second virtual display.


**Amended 2026-11-17: a reconnect waits for its own lane.** Seen on GitHub's Linux runner with the API 35 emulator (d5a8392, `text.steps`): during `type "hello from CI"` the video stream ended, the daemon dropped the connection and asked again at once, and the agent answered `lane in use: the phone's own screen`, so the step failed with `lane the phone's own screen of this device is already open from this daemon` although the text had been typed. The agent frees a lane when the thread of the connection that held it sees its socket close, and a thread busy with an action sees that only when the action ends. The agent belongs to one daemon (its owner file), so a lane it holds at a reconnect is that daemon's dropped connection: `Agent::start` now asks again every 100 ms for up to 5 s before it refuses. Unit test: `a_lane_this_daemon_still_holds_is_waited_for_and_other_refusals_are_not`. On the API 37 emulator, twenty reconnects right after `daemon stop` answered (fifteen before the change, five after; the race does not show from a new daemon, which takes longer to start), and `text.steps` replayed. Why the video stream ended on the runner is not known; the Action now keeps the daemon's log for that.
## D74. A screenshot waits for the frames the encoder still holds, and a hidden display on a software encoder is lighter

**Choice.** Three changes to one path. (1) The number of a `video_barrier` travels as `barrier`, not as `id`. (2) The agent holds a barrier while its encoder is behind the screen: when the last frame it wrote came out more than 100 ms after its presentation time, the barrier waits until a frame presented after the request has been written, or until the encoder has given nothing for 100 ms plus twice that lateness. An encoder that keeps up is not waited for. The reply says how much longer than usual the barrier may take (`wait_ms`), and the host adds it to its 1.5 s. (3) A hidden display whose encoder is software only is opened at two pixels a point and 15 frames a second.

**Why.** D73's open defect, followed to its cause (`docs/spikes/video-lag.md`). `Control::request` stamps every request with its own `id`, so the barrier's number, sent as `id` since 06bab61, was overwritten; the agent wrote back the request's id, always the larger, and the host, which waits for a barrier at or above its own number, stopped waiting from a connection's second screenshot on. With that mended a screenshot was still wrong, because the agent wrote the barrier after the output on hand and the frames inside the encoder followed it: up to 1.45 s of them with two lanes animating. And a software encoder cannot keep up with a phone panel at 30 frames a second at all (median 598 ms behind for one stream), while at two pixels a point and 15 frames a second it can (43 ms). A screenshot is what an agent decides on; one that shows the screen before the action it follows is worse than a slow one.

**Rejected.** A fixed pause before a screenshot: it is paid on every phone and is wrong whenever the encoder is further behind than the pause. Waiting on every screenshot for a frame newer than the request: on an encoder that keeps up it costs up to the 100 ms repeat interval and buys nothing. One estimate of the encode time for the whole process, and letting the barrier go after twice that with no output: built first and dropped, it let go during a 216 ms stall with the estimate at 77 ms. Suspending the encoder's input to shed the queue (`PARAMETER_KEY_SUSPEND`): the frames it discards can include the last one, and then the stream stays wrong until the screen changes again; not tried. Reading the screenshot from the display instead of the stream when the encoder is behind: a second path with its own permissions and timing; not tried. The lighter stream for the phone's own screen on a software encoder too: recordings come from that stream.

**Evidence.** API 37 emulator, daemon on the Mac mini (debug build), guest disk quiet. A screenshot of lane `b` right after each of five taps into a Settings page, lane `a` tapping at the same moment; the page and the list differ by 40 KB as PNG.

| Run | Showed the page | Time for the screenshot |
|---|---|---|
| Two lanes, before | 1 of 5, 1 of 5, 0 of 5 | 0.21 to 0.40 s |
| Two lanes and the phone's screen, before | 1 of 10 | |
| Two lanes, after | 5 of 5 | 0.61 to 1.11 s |
| Two lanes and the phone's screen showing Clock, after | 5 of 5 | 1.41 to 1.73 s |
| The phone's own screen alone, after | 5 of 5 | 0.48 to 0.74 s after a tap, 0.15 to 0.21 s on a still screen |

Of the fifteen screenshots after, eight are byte-identical to the one taken two seconds later and the rest differ by under 300 bytes. Every tap landed (the testbed logged `b1` to `b5` in each run). Tests: `a_barrier_request_carries_its_number_apart_from_the_requests_own_id` (failed with `left: Null, right: 700`), `a_barrier_the_agent_says_is_behind_is_waited_for_that_much_longer` (failed with `DeviceGone`).

**Limits.** One emulator, one software encoder; no physical phone, so the rule for an encoder that keeps up is what a hardware encoder gets, and that it does keep up is not measured here. The presentation time was compared with `System.nanoTime` on API 37 only; an encoder whose times are more than 60 s off is treated as untimed and gets the barrier as before. An encoder that was on time at its last frame and then stalls with newer frames inside is not waited for; not seen in the fifteen screenshots. On an emulator a screenshot right after an action now takes about half a second on the phone's own screen and up to 1.7 s with three streams, where it took 0.2 s and was often wrong; one of the phone's own screen with a running clock under three streams took 3.49 s. Releases 0.1.2 to 0.1.4 have the barrier that does not wait. The published screenshot time (9.1 ms p50 on the emulator, `docs/bench/speed.md`, run of 2026-11-11) was measured with that barrier, so it is the time to hand over the frame on hand; with the barrier waited for, a screenshot of a still screen took 120 to 126 ms in the daemon here against 106 to 111 ms before (debug build, two tunnels), because the agent looks for a barrier request every 10 ms, and one right after an action on an emulator waits for the encoder. The benchmark has not been run again. A recording of a lane on an emulator is 822x1846 at 15 frames a second.

**Reopen if.** A phone's hardware encoder stamps frames on another clock; a screenshot is again seen older than the reply before it.

**Next, not built.** Two changes would give an emulator back the fast screenshot, and neither could be measured: win-pc restarted during the session and its disk stayed saturated afterwards. (1) The phone's own screen on a software encoder takes the same lighter stream as a hidden display; a spike build of it was made and not run. An emulator's recording would then be 822x1846 at 15 frames a second, which is about the rate the software encoder reached at full size anyway. (2) A barrier asked of an encoder that keeps up is written by the control thread at once, under a lock shared with the frame writer, and not at the frame writer's next 10 ms poll.


## D75. `init` adds the server to every agent on the machine and then runs the doctor checks

**Choice.** `phonedriver init` (`crates/cli/src/init.rs`) looks for Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, VS Code and Gemini CLI, adds the MCP server to each one found, prints one line for each, and runs the doctor checks. Claude Code (`claude mcp add --scope user`) and Codex (`codex mcp get`, then `codex mcp add`) are asked through their own commands. Cursor, VS Code and Gemini CLI get the entry in their JSON file, written to a file beside it and renamed. Nothing is replaced: an agent that has the entry says `already there`, a file with another `phonedriver` entry or with anything that is not plain JSON is left as it is and the entry is printed. The npm launcher sets `PHONEDRIVER_NPX`, and then the entry is `npx -y phonedriver mcp`; otherwise it is the binary, by name when that name on `PATH` is this file.

**Why.** Until now a new user ran one command for each agent, each with its own syntax, from a page of the site. A competitor (argent, Software Mansion) installs with one `npx ... init`, and that is the first thing a new user compares. The server already sends its own instructions over MCP, so nothing needs to be copied into a project.

**Rejected.** Writing Claude Code's `~/.claude.json` or Codex's `config.toml` directly: both clients rewrite those files themselves, and their own commands exist. `code --add-mcp`: it can open a window, and it says nothing about an entry that is already there. A wizard with questions: an agent runs this command too, and there is nothing to choose. Installing skills or rules into the project: the MCP instructions carry them. Replacing a differing entry: it may be the user's own build. Editing JSON with comments: it needs a parser that keeps them, for a file the user can paste five lines into.

**Evidence.** Eight tests in `crates/cli/tests/init.rs` with stand-in `claude` and `codex` programs and a home of their own (first run `unrecognized subcommand 'init'`), two unit tests, one launcher test. On the Mac mini with the real Claude Code and a throwaway home: `Claude Code  added, for every project`, then `already there` on the second run, and `claude`'s own file holds `{'phonedriver': {'type': 'stdio', 'command': '.../phonedriver', 'args': ['mcp'], 'env': {}}}`. `claude` and `codex` were first tried by hand in a throwaway home on the MacBook: a second `claude mcp add` exits 1 with `already exists in user config`; `codex mcp get` exits 1 with `No MCP server named 'phonedriver' found`.

**Limits.** Codex was run by hand and through a stand-in, not through `init` itself. Cursor, VS Code and Gemini CLI were not started to see that they load the entry; the file locations and keys are from each client's documentation as read on 14 November 2026. Windows was not run. The keys of an edited file are written back in alphabetical order. Zed, Cline and Windsurf are not handled. `npx -y phonedriver init` exists from the release after 0.1.4.

**Reopen if.** A client moves its file or changes its key; users ask for a project-scoped entry; a client's own command gains a way to say that an entry exists.

## D76. `compare` says what changed between two screenshots, as text

**Choice.** `phonedriver compare <before.png> [after.png]` and the MCP `screenshot` tool's `compare: true` answer `same`, or the regions whose pixels differ and the texts that differ inside them, each with a point to tap (`crates/cli/src/compare.rs`). Pixels first: cells of 8 by 8 pixels, a cell has changed when its mean brightness difference is above 4 of 255, changed cells within three cells of each other are one region, regions whose bounds overlap are joined. Text second, and only when a region exists: both pictures are read with the recognition of section 9, a line is paired with the line that overlaps it by more than half, and a pair is reported only when its letters and digits differ. Over MCP the server keeps the last screenshot it returned and compares with that; a `compare` call's own picture becomes the next "before". `--image` writes the later picture with the regions boxed, through the drawing `screenshot --marks` uses.

**Why.** On a screen with no element list (a game, a canvas, a Flutter view) the only way to see what an action did was a second picture. A picture of the iPhone 17 Simulator's screen is 115,912 bytes of tool result; `same` is 32 bytes and a full page change is 563. A competitor (argent) has visual comparison for app developers' regression tests; an agent needs the smaller thing, the answer to "did that do anything, and what".

**Rejected.** A comparison of the two text readings alone: recognition differs between two pictures of one unchanged screen (the Fast level read `9:20` and then `9:20 •`), so every call would report something; pixels decide whether anything changed. A per-pixel difference image as the reply: it is a picture again. A daemon method: two files need no device, and the MCP server already holds the picture it returned. A threshold from theory: it is from thirteen real pairs (below). Hiding the status bar: the host does not know its bounds on a screen with no element list.

**Evidence.** Thirteen pairs of real screenshots from the API 37 emulator, read with a throwaway script before the code: on pairs of one unchanged screen the largest cell difference was 2.6, on pairs with a real change the smallest changed cell was 9.3; the threshold is 4. Seven unit tests in `compare.rs`; the last one, `a_change_inside_the_bounds_of_another_is_reported_with_it`, was written after the live run below reported three regions with two of them inside the first, and failed with `left: [Rect { x: 40.0, y: 40.0, w: 240.0, h: 240.0 }, Rect { x: 152.0, y: 152.0, w: 16.0, h: 16.0 }]`. `--test cli compare_of_two_files...` and `--test mcp compare_needs_an_earlier_screenshot...` pass; `--test mcp` 45 passed, the tool-list budget test among them. Nine real pairs on the Mac mini: two unchanged pairs `same` in 0.04 s; a light-to-dark switch of one page `~ "Will turn on when Bedtime starts" → "Will never turn off automatically" at 132,545`; a pressed row `region 16,232 384x80 (no text changed)`.
