- The developer disk image: `phonedriver ios setup --download-ddi` downloads Xcode 27.2's personalized image (three files) from `doronz88/DeveloperDiskImage` at commit `dcec539` with `curl`, checks pinned SHA-256 hashes and keeps it under the home; `--ddi <folder>` uses files copied from a Mac instead. idevice's `mount_personalized` gets the ticket from Apple's TSS server, over HTTPS and without following redirects from 0.1.7 (D106; plain HTTP before). Each connect mounts the kept image again when a restart has unmounted it.
- No signing team is asked for, so `blocker` reports none off a Mac. `doctor` reports usbmuxd and whether the runner is installed, in place of the team and the profile's expiry. `UserAction::InstallRunner` says what to do when the runner is missing.

**Why.** A contributor's iPhone is on iOS 26.5 and their computer runs Windows. The CoreDevice tier needs iOS 27 (spike I2: "Remote control requires iOS 27.0 or later"), so on 26 the runner is the only tier, and only Xcode can compile it. idevice 0.1.68, already a dependency, has the XCTest launcher and the personalized mount (`tss` feature), so the Windows side needs no new crates. The contributor chose Sideloadly over signing inside phonedriver (which would mean handling an Apple ID password and two-factor codes) and over a paid developer account, and approved the download from `doronz88/DeveloperDiskImage` for their own use (2026-11-20). The maintainer approved offering it (2026-11-20; D9 amended): the files are Apple's, fetched over HTTPS from a pinned commit and checked against pinned SHA-256 hashes, and a device mounts only an image Apple personalized for it. `--ddi <folder>` with the three files from a Mac's Xcode works without it.

**Rejected.** Signing inside phonedriver with an Apple ID login, as AltServer does: it handles the person's Apple credentials. Using Appium's prebuilt WebDriverAgent: the host speaks the runner's own protocol. Cross-compiling the runner on Windows: XCTest's private headers and frameworks come only with Xcode.

**Evidence.** Windows 11, 2026-11-20: `cargo build --release -p phonedriver --no-default-features` and clippy with `-D warnings` on `phonedriver-ios`, `phonedriver-core` (all targets) and `phonedriver` (lib and bin) are clean; `cargo test --release -p phonedriver-ios -p phonedriver-core --lib` passes 226 and 83, among them the new tests for the pinned download addresses, the hash check of a kept image, a `--ddi` folder missing a file, and finding the runner by its executable under another bundle id. With no device attached, `phonedriver ios setup` prints `need  a device: ...` and `phonedriver doctor` prints `ok    ios usbmuxd  answers; 0 iPhone or iPad attached` against Apple Devices' usbmuxd.

Run on one device, an iPhone17,5 on iOS 26.5 over USB from Windows 11 (2026-11-20, `progress.md`): the mount, the XCTest launch of a Sideloadly-signed runner whose bundle id Sideloadly had changed, `snapshot` (184 ms), `screenshot`, `tap`, `type`, `launch` and `press back`. With Developer Mode off the device answers a mount with only `ImageMountFailed`, so `setup::mount_blocked` stops setup and the daemon before the mount and names Developer Mode.

**Limits.** One device and one iOS version so far. Taps take 1.3 to 1.5 s against 316 ms on the Simulator: about 230 ms of input confirmation, a settle of 0.5 to 0.9 s on polled screenshots and 0.2 to 0.5 s of `stabilize`. On a screen with an endless spinner (Settings > Bluetooth while it scans) every action spends the full `LOADING_WAIT` of 4 s in `stabilize` (a 5.6 s tap in the trace). Off a Mac the device must be on the cable (`ios wifi on` still needs Xcode); D103 lifts this. The build without the `android` feature also needed a fix in `commands::command`, which named `pair` and `untether` for clap even when they do not exist.

## D97. An MCP session ends with its connection, and Android field values are their text (2026-11-19)

Fixes from an outside review of 0.1.5 (#15 and #16), recorded together because each changes a rule the code kept silently.

**An MCP connection that closes takes its session's state with it.** The daemon releases its leases, drops its state on every device (last screen, journal, approvals), closes its trace file and forgets it as an agent. An MCP server's session is `mcp-<pid>` and never returns; until now its state stayed while the daemon lived. A command-line session keeps its state, because each command is its own connection and `save` comes after the run. The MCP server reconnects only after `-32603`, which only the client produces, when the daemon is gone and the state with it.

**`hello` carries the session.** The MCP server's `hello` named no session, so the daemon registered `default` as the agent and the server's calls (`mcp-<pid>`) got the 30-second terminal hold instead of the agent lease (#14, fixed by the reporter in #19).

**An editable Android field's value is its text.** Its state description is not read as text: Compose gives an empty field the state description "Empty", which showed as `textbox "Empty"` and made clearing a field fail verification. Non-editable nodes (switches, progress bars) keep their state description. Cost: an editable field's state description (an error state) is not shown.

**A tap is at most 10 taps and a hold at most 10 seconds,** checked at the daemon for `act` and every `batch` step: larger values allocated a gesture per tap and overflowed the timing.

**`devices {use: "<id>"}`** sets the MCP server's default device for the rest of the session (#16); `device` on one call still overrides.

**A web page's fixed bottom bar covers what is under it** (#16). Chrome lists it as an unlabeled group, gives no draw order, and clips a tile's bounds to the web view rather than to the bar, so a tap on a tile under the bar hit the bar and reported success. A group at least 80% of the web view's width, at most half its height, flush with its bottom and with every descendant showing is kept and counts as a covering bar, like iOS's `Toolbar`. A tap goes to the middle of the part of the target that shows beside the bar when at least 12 pt shows, and fails `OffScreen` otherwise; `scroll --until` keeps going while the target is under it. Cost: a page's last block with that shape and nothing hidden is read as a bar too; it only changes where a tap on something overlapping it lands. Reopen if Chrome starts reporting draw order or a fixed position.

## D98. A saved secret names an environment variable, and a saved run may hold `{{name}}` (2026-11-20)

A run with a login could not replay in CI: D60 kept a typed secret out of the file as a `manual` step, and the replay stopped there for a person. A run that signs up could not replay twice: the same typed e-mail address is taken the second time. This adds two things to `save` and `replay`, with no change to the daemon's wire.

**Secrets.** The journal keeps a typed secret as the `type` action without its `text` (`secret: true`, target kept as for any step). `save` gives each such step `secret_env`, `PHONEDRIVER_SECRET_1`, `_2`, ... in the order of the file, and its summary says which variables to set. `replay` reads the variable from the environment of the process that runs it, puts it in the action as `text` just before the `act` call, and sends it as a secret, so D16 (traces hold a length) and D70 (masked in every reply and error, remembered by the daemon) apply as for `type --secret`. The replay runs in the client: the CLI process, or for MCP the MCP server, whose `env` is the user's configuration. No tool argument can carry a secret, and `vars` is for plain values. A variable that is not set, or is empty, stops the replay at that step with the variable named in the message and the hint, as a `manual` step did. A file from before (`{"manual": ...}`) still stops there. The name must begin `PHONEDRIVER_SECRET_`: a steps file is text an agent can edit, and without the prefix an edited file could type `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` into a field the agent can then screenshot (the masking covers replies, not pixels). A person may rename within the prefix. The prefix limits which variables, not where they go: since D105 a replayed secret is typed only into a password or one-time code field unless `PHONEDRIVER_REPLAY_SECRET_ANY_FIELD=1` is set where the replay runs. A shell command given with `secret` stays a `manual` step: a command is not a field's text, and nothing observed asked for it.

**Variables.** `--var name=value` (repeatable; MCP `vars`) and the built-in `{{run}}`, an 8-digit hex id from the clock and the process id, the same in every step of one replay and different in the next. `{{name}}` is replaced in every string of a step's `params`: typed text, `open` URLs, `shell` commands and targets' text. Targets are included so a step can tap what an earlier step typed (`tap "test+{{run}}@example.com"`); one rule is easier to say than a list, and `summary`, `manual` and `secret_env` are not touched. A name is letters, digits, `_`, `-`, `.`; other text between braces stays as it is, and a value is not scanned again, so `--var x='{{x}}'` types braces and there is no escape syntax. Before the first step, the steps that will run (from `--from`) are scanned, and every name with no value is refused at once, so a CI job does not fail at step 9 of 12. `--var run=...` is refused: a name that sometimes means a fresh id would make a replay's data depend on a flag. Variables are not secrets and show in replies and failure messages; the docs say so and point to `secret_env`. `save` never writes one, so a person decides what varies. The failure message and the step list show the step with its values filled in, so a failing step reads as it ran. The replay's first line says `(run <id>)` when the file uses `{{run}}`.

**Action.** `action.yml` needs no change for secrets: a composite action's steps inherit the `env` of the step that uses it (the replay step's own `env:` adds to it), and the `phonedriver replay` there is the process that reads the variable. A `vars` input, one `name=value` a line, passes `--var` to each replay, because without it only `{{run}}` could be used in CI.

**Rejected.** Reading the secret in the daemon from its own environment: the daemon outlives the CI step and is not where the user sets variables. Writing the secret to a file the user names: a file of secrets next to a file of steps is how they get committed. A `--secret NAME=ENV` flag on `replay`: renaming is a file edit already. Variable names for any `{{...}}` in the environment (`{{env.X}}`): it would be a second way to a secret that is not masked.

**Evidence.** `progress.md`, 2026-11-20.

**Limits.** No escape for a literal `{{name}}` other than the value trick above. A secret typed into a field the app does not confirm is checked by length only, as for `type --secret`. `vars` in the Action are visible in the job log; only `env` from `secrets.*` is masked by GitHub.

**Amended 2026-11-22 (#41).** Two alarm replays were given `day1: "Tue"` where the buttons say `Tuesday`, and stopped there. A blank may hold an example after a colon, `{{day1:Tuesday}}`: the name is what is before it, and the "needs" message shows the example (`day1 (like "Tuesday")`). The example is never typed; a blank without a value is still refused. Old versions read `{{day1:Tuesday}}` as text, not a name, and type it as it is.

## D99. The device's networks are read by one call, and `wait` follows the VPN and the log (2026-11-20, #17)

**Choice.** Three things came out of testing a VPN app (issue #17), and a fourth was declined.

- `network` (daemon method, CLI verb, MCP tool; read-only, so no approval) returns the networks `dumpsys connectivity` lists as connected, with transports, the system default network, interface and DNS servers, and for a VPN its owning package (`ni{VPN CONNECTED extra: VPN:<package>}`), the user id ranges it carries (`Uids: <{0-99999}>`) and the network under it. The always-on VPN app and its lockdown come from `settings get secure always_on_vpn_app` and `always_on_vpn_lockdown`, private DNS from `settings get global private_dns_mode` and `private_dns_specifier`, in the same shell call as the dump. `Device::network` has an Android implementation; everything else keeps the default `Unsupported`. A field the platform did not give is named in `missing` and left empty, never filled with Android's default: an unset `private_dns_mode` reads `null`, which is not "opportunistic" to this call. A `settings` answer of `null` for the always-on app or lockdown is an answer (none, off). A network that is not `CONNECTED` is not listed, so a VPN that is still connecting is not up.
- `wait vpn:up` and `wait vpn:down` read `Device::network` once a second until the VPN network is there or gone. A dump that did not list the networks (`missing` holds `networks`) answers neither; the wait goes on, and its timeout says so (0.1.7, D107). The user ids and underlying networks are read after the VPN's `TransportInfo`, whose session name the VPN app chooses. A timeout names the last state (`last seen: cellular, wifi (default)`). The poll is on a timer because the shell has no event for it; the tree waits are not.
- `wait log:<text>` (`A|B` for either, any case; `--app` or MCP `app`) is `Device::log_wait`: the device streams its own log from now on and the first line with one of the words is returned and quoted in the summary. Android: `logcat -v brief -T "$(date +%s.%N)"` over an adb shell stream from the device's own clock, with `--uid=<the app's uid>` when an app is named (the uid, as `logs` does, so a restarted app is still read). The iOS Simulator: `log stream --style compact --level debug`, run through `shell_args` like `shell` (D21 and its 2026-11 amendment), with `--predicate 'processImagePath CONTAINS "/<Name>.app/"'` for an app. A physical iPhone or iPad: `Unsupported`. A timeout says how many lines arrived, none matching. The reply carries the summary and the screen's header and no screen text; it is masked by the daemon's one mask (D70), and the saved step for the wait keeps no log text.
- `com.android.vpndialogs` is a system dialog package (`core::alert`), so the "Connection request" dialog is an alert in the header and `tap OK` answers it. On an emulator `shell "appops set <package> ACTIVATE_VPN allow"` pre-authorises an app and the dialog never appears; that is documented and not a flag.
- Declined: an egress probe built in. phonedriver makes no outbound connection of its own (D9); a test app that makes the request and logs what the endpoint saw, read with `wait log:`, keeps that line.

**Why.** An agent testing a VPN needs to know whether the tunnel is up, whose traffic it carries and what DNS says, and the only way before was `shell "dumpsys connectivity | grep ..."` over 340 lines of platform text. `wait` on a log line is useful beyond VPNs (a benchmark job reads a code out of the log).

**Rejected.** A new capability bit for network or log waits: `Unsupported` from the default method says it, as `logs` and `page` do. One `ConnectivityManager` callback through the agent for `vpn:` instead of a poll: it would add a message to the agent's protocol for a wait that costs one adb shell a second. Reading private DNS's per-network `PrivateDnsServerName` from the link properties: it is absent when private DNS is off, so it cannot say why. Returning the whole screen from `wait vpn:`/`wait log:`: nothing on the screen is what the agent waited for.

**Evidence.** API 37 emulator, 2026-11-20, with a throwaway VPN app (a `VpnService` that opens a `tun0` for `10.77.0.0/24`): `network` printed the VPN as `102 wifi+vpn of dev.pd016.vpnprobe on tun0 for uids 0-99999 over 101 · dns 10.77.0.1`; `wait vpn:up` returned as the app connected after a force-stop and `wait vpn:down` as it was stopped; with no VPN `wait vpn:up --timeout 3000` timed out with `last seen: cellular, wifi (default)`; `wait log:I/pd016` returned `I/pd016   ( 5750): pd-016-check` when `adb shell log -t pd016 pd-016-check` ran from another shell; `--app Calendar` returned a Calendar line when the app was started. The dialog with `appops ... default` came up as `alert com.android.vpndialogs: "Connection request"` and with `allow` did not. Fixtures: `crates/android/tests/fixtures/dumpsys_connectivity_*.txt` (a real dump with and without the VPN) and `crates/core/tests/fixtures/android/vpn_consent_dialog.json`.

**Limits.** Android only for `network` and `vpn:`; the dump's wording is the platform's and was read on API 37 only (a `Uids` range, the `extra: VPN:<package>` and `OwnerUid` were seen; older releases print the VPN's package differently or not at all, and then the owner is named as not given). The Simulator's `log stream` was not run live. A physical Android phone was not touched; `dumpsys connectivity` took 6 to 10 s for a minute after a VPN app was force-stopped, so the read is allowed 20 s and fails with a plain error if it does not answer. A log pattern also matches lines about itself: a `log -t tag pattern` shell command is itself logged by `adbd`. The poll and the stream do not survive the daemon restarting.

**Reopen if.** A VPN test needs the exact moment of a change rather than one second's resolution; the platform's `dumpsys` wording changes and `missing` fills on a device that has the data; someone needs the log wait on a physical iPhone.

## D100. `apps notify` sends a push notification to an app on the Simulator, and an app under test on a physical phone can be named by the person (2026-11-20)

**`apps notify <app> '<json>'`** (`--file payload.json`; MCP `apps` with `action: "notify"` and `payload`) delivers a push notification to an installed app, so an agent can test what the app does with one. The app resolves by name or id like the other `apps` operations. The reply is `sent a push notification to <app>`.

**iOS Simulator.** `xcrun simctl push <udid> <bundle id> -` with the payload on standard input: the Simulator delivers it as Apple's push service would, and the app's notification delegate runs. Before sending, the payload must be a JSON object with an `aps` object, or it is refused (`refused`, -32025) with what is wrong and "nothing was sent"; `simctl` itself accepts less and fails later with a less useful message. Seen on an iOS 26.5 Simulator with the iOS testbed: after the permission was allowed, `apps notify Testbed '{"aps":{"alert":{"title":"Order shipped","body":"Your pad thai is on its way"}},"order":"7"}'` showed the banner (looked at in a screenshot) and the testbed's event log gained `{"ev":"notification","state":"shown"}`.

**Physical iPhone.** `unsupported`: only Apple's push service, with the app's own certificate or key, can push to a device, and phonedriver holds neither.

**Android.** `unsupported`, with a reason that says what does and does not work. Reasoning, not a device run: Firebase Cloud Messaging reaches an app as a broadcast from Google Play services to a receiver that is protected by a signature permission only that service holds, and the shell user does not hold it. `cmd notification post` does work, but it posts a notification from the shell's own package; it shows how a notification looks and never reaches the app's own code, so presenting it as a push test would be a false pass. Two ways that might work on a debuggable build (`run-as <package>` and a broadcast to the app's own receiver; `adb root` on an emulator) depend on the app's own receiver and its message format, so they are not promised by this command; `shell` is there for them. Reopen if a way is found that delivers a real FCM data message to an app with no changes to the app.

**Approval.** Not guarded, like `grant`, `revoke` and `clear`. D27 asks about a tap that spends money or cannot be undone; a notification does neither, and the call is an `apps` operation, not a tap on a screen element, which is what the guard reads. It changes what the app shows or logs, not what the person owns, and it runs only on a Simulator, a throwaway device. The app may act on a payload (a deep link, a badge), but so can any `open` or tap the agent makes, and those taps are guarded by what they press.

**An app under test on a physical phone (#18, #17 item 5).** On a physical Android phone `apps clear`, `uninstall`, `grant`, `revoke` and `launch --grant` touched only apps in the install record (D19), so an app that Gradle or Android Studio installed could not be used with them. `PHONEDRIVER_APPS_UNDER_TEST=com.example.app,com.example.other` names packages that count as installed by the person for those five. The CLI reads it when it starts the daemon (`phonedriver daemon`, which the CLI and the MCP server start with their own environment, as for `PHONEDRIVER_TRACE_FULL`) and passes the list to `Daemon::start`; `installed_by_us` answers yes for a listed package before it looks at the record. A running daemon keeps the list it started with, so the refusal says to run `phonedriver daemon stop` first. A listed package is never written to the install record, and the record behaves as before (an app phonedriver installed is still cleared, and its entry still ends with its uninstall). The refusal for an app outside both names the variable, so a developer finds it. `shell` is left as it was: its refusal on a physical device (D21) reads the install record only and `force` is its way past. Since 0.1.7 (D107) it also compares the install time for the packages a command names, as `installed_by_us` does, so `shell "pm clear"` refuses an app its owner installed again like `apps clear` does; the variable still does not open `shell`.

**Threat model.** D21's filter is a guard against mistakes: it keeps an agent that misjudges a package name from wiping a banking app or removing something on a phone it was lent. The variable keeps that, because the list is something the person writes in the MCP server's `env` or their shell, never a tool argument: no MCP call, no `apps` argument and no step in a replay file can add a package, so an agent that is steered by screen text cannot widen what it may wipe. It does not stop an agent that has a shell on the person's own computer (it can start its own daemon with the variable set, as it could with `PHONEDRIVER_GUARD=off`, D27), and a person who lists a package has said they accept the loss of that app's data. The cost is one more place where a mistake is possible: a package listed by habit stays wipeable for every agent that uses that daemon.

## D101. A device list the daemon enforces, and a `doctor` that probes only devices nobody else owns (2026-11-20, #26, #27)

**A device list, enforced where devices are resolved (#26).** `PHONEDRIVER_ALLOW_DEVICES` and `PHONEDRIVER_DENY_DEVICES` (comma lists, read when the daemon starts, like D100's variable) and the file `devices` in the phonedriver home (`~/.phonedriver/devices`, or under `PHONEDRIVER_HOME`; `allow <id>` and `deny <id>` lines, `#` comments, read again whenever its size or modification time changes). One wrapper around the daemon's device source, `Gated` in `daemon/policy.rs`, filters `list`, `list_for` and `off` and refuses `connect`, `connect_on` and `boot` (a `boot` name is matched against the filtered `off` list before anything starts, since 0.1.7, D107), so listing, name resolution (`--device`, `PHONEDRIVER_DEVICE`, `devices {use}`, a lane), `devices`, replay and the Action all see a hidden device as not connected, and `--force` is an argument of calls that never get that far. The existing not-found error is the answer; its list of connected devices leaves the hidden ones out.

**How the lists combine.** A device is hidden when any deny entry, from the environment or the file, names it; and, for each place that has an allow list, when that list does not name it. Deny always wins, and a machine-wide file's allow list cannot be widened by an MCP client's `env`, which the file's author did not write. An unset or empty variable is no list. The file is in the phonedriver home and not a fixed `~/.phonedriver` because the home is what already names this daemon's socket, ledger and traces (`PHONEDRIVER_HOME` runs a separate daemon, and tests need one), and a machine-wide setting is the default home.

**What an entry names.** The id as `devices` shows it (`android:emulator-5554`, `android:192.168.1.20:5555`, `ios:<udid>`, `sim:<udid>`), with or without the prefix (an exact match, not `--device`'s suffix match, so `5555` does not name every device on that port), or an Android phone's hardware serial (`ro.serialno`), with or without `android:`, which names the phone over USB and over Wi-Fi alike. The serial is read with one read-only `getprop ro.serialno` through the adb server (the property the install record keys on; the one-per-phone folding takes a name without a colon, such as an emulator's, as its own serial and so does not read it, which is why the list always reads the property; `AdbSource` keeps the answer per connection), and only when an entry could name a serial (not an `ios:` or `sim:` id, an emulator's name or an address) and the id did not already settle it: a deny entry that names the id hides it without a read, an allow entry that names the id admits it without one. Nothing is pushed. Emulators of one SDK image all report the same `ro.serialno` (both emulators here said `EMULATOR37X2X13X0`), so a serial entry hides every one of them: name an emulator by its id. A read that fails hides the device from a deny list (the rule cannot be shown not to apply) and does not admit it to an allow list.

**A list that cannot be read hides everything.** A line that is neither `allow <id>` nor `deny <id>`, or a file that exists and cannot be read, makes `devices`, every device call and `hidden` fail with an error that names the file and the line, because a typo such as `deney` must not leave a phone open. No file is no list.

**The reply on a terminal says so; the agent's does not.** `phonedriver devices` ends with `1 device hidden by the device list` (the `hidden` daemon method, which `doctor` also uses and which is not an MCP tool), so a person can tell why a phone is missing. The MCP `devices` reply says nothing: to the agent a hidden phone is not connected, and naming it invites a workaround. `SKILL.md` tells the agent that a phone that is plugged in and never listed may be kept out on purpose, and to tell the user.

**Limits.** A running daemon keeps the environment it started with (the file is the form that takes effect at once). A client whose environment differs from the running daemon's gets the daemon's list. A device that was driven before it was hidden keeps its connection until the daemon stops; its next call fails. `pair`, `untether` and `ios setup`, `ios wifi` and `ios register` act on a device the person names without the daemon, and are not covered. The list is the person's setting: an agent with a shell on the computer can start its own daemon under another `PHONEDRIVER_HOME` without it (D100's threat model).

**`doctor` leaves devices alone that nobody named (#27).** Before this, `doctor` without `--device` pushed and started the agent on every device adb listed, which replaced the agent another session used, and only said which devices on stderr first (D34's owner file stops a second daemon, not a probe from the first). Now, without `--device`, it checks the host (adb, the adb server, Xcode, the simulator list, the socket) and lists every device, and it probes (starts the agent or the runner) only devices that belong to nobody: an emulator or a Simulator that no live session holds. A physical phone or tablet, Android or iOS, is listed with `not probed: a physical device, which doctor leaves alone unless asked` and the command `phonedriver doctor --device <id>`; a device a live session holds is listed with the session's name and the same command. `doctor --all-devices` probes physical devices too, still skipping devices another live session holds; `--device <id>` probes exactly that device and nothing else, held or not (the probe shares the lease, as before). `init` runs the default form.

**Emulators are still probed by default.** They are nobody's property, a first-time user of `init` on an emulator should see the agent come up, and the cost of a wrong probe is a restarted emulator agent, not a person's phone. "Physical" is the id: `android:emulator-*`, `sim:*` are not, everything else Android and every `ios:` is.

**What "held by a live session" is.** The `devices` reply now has `lease_live` for each leased device: true for an MCP session's lease, and for a command-line session's within `TERMINAL_HOLD` (30 s) of its last call, the same rule that decides whether another session may take the device. A lease that has outlived its hold does not count, so `phonedriver snapshot` followed by `phonedriver doctor` a minute later probes the emulator. If the daemon cannot be asked, nothing is probed without `--device` and doctor says so.

**Rejected.** A hard list in the CLI process (the daemon's environment decides, not each client's). Matching by `--device`'s suffix rule (`5555` would name every device on that port). Failing open on an unreadable file. Telling the agent about hidden devices. Probing physical iPhones for pairing and Developer Mode by default (those are reads, but they wake the lockdown service on a phone nobody named; `ios setup` is where a first-time iPhone user goes).

## D102. An app scope the daemon enforces: `PHONEDRIVER_APP_SCOPE` (2026-11-20, #28)

**The scope.** `PHONEDRIVER_APP_SCOPE=com.example.app,com.example.other`, read when the daemon starts (as `PHONEDRIVER_APPS_UNDER_TEST` is, D100), never a tool argument. It is a different thing from D100's variable: that one says which apps phonedriver may clear or uninstall on a physical phone; this one says which apps the agent may see or touch at all. A list that names no package (`,`) stops the daemon with an error, so a typo does not turn the scope off.

**One seam.** `Actor::ensure_live` wraps the connected device in `ScopedDevice` (`daemon/scope.rs`), a second `Device` implementation over any device. Every verb, batch step, `then` path, replay and trace reaches the device through it, so no verb can forget the rule. `core::scope::AppScope::redact` takes the raw tree and removes the nodes of every window whose package is not in the scope, the status and navigation bars, and any node whose window the tree does not list; the withheld windows go in `RawTree::outside`. The header's `app` line then names the first of them with `(outside the app scope)` and a hint. Reads need no other change, because the rendered screen, the traces and the error hints are built from the redacted tree.

**Implicit in scope.** Alert windows (`core::alert`, which includes the VPN consent dialog), `input_method` windows, and the bars (since 0.1.7 only the system UI's, `com.android.systemui`, D107). The share sheet is not: a window with `android:id/resolver_list` or from `com.android.intentresolver` is withheld even where `core::alert` calls it an alert, because a share sheet lists contacts. The home screen gets no special case: it is an app outside the scope, so `press home` works and its widgets and app list are never shown. A window thinner than 5 percent of the screen on one side that is not an application is an edge strip (the HONOR X8's `gesture` windows), not content.

**Rules in the layer.** Gesture, key, text, clipboard set, page and the buttons other than home, back and lock are refused while any withheld window is on screen: one rule, not "the front window", because a heads-up or a split-screen app can be under the finger while the app in scope is "in front". Recents and notifications buttons, `clipboard get`, `record`, `capture_tree` and `shell` are refused outright. `screenshot` is refused when a withheld window is on screen, checked before and after the capture; blanking by window bounds was not chosen because the picture holds the status bar and the keyboard strip and the bounds of a rounded or animating window are not exact. `launch`, `grant`, `revoke`, `push`, `app_op`, `logs` and `log_wait` refuse an app outside the scope, `apps` is filtered, `open` needs an app in the scope. The actor turns "no app named X" into a refusal that names the scope, and checks the screen before an action so the refusal says why (a `type` in another app would otherwise say "no field has focus"). `network` hides the owner of a VPN outside the scope. `setting` and `install` pass through.

**iOS.** The runner sees the front app and SpringBoard alerts; a banner or Control Center is invisible to it. So under a scope an iPhone or Simulator takes no screenshot (ids `ios:` and `sim:`), and a window with no bundle id is outside. Since 0.1.7 (D107) it opens no link, and `press back` and hiding the keyboard, which tap the front app there, are refused while an app outside the scope is in front. Reads and actions are gated by the front app's bundle id like Android's packages. Not run on a device in this change; the rule is tested on the fake device.

**A scope the daemon does not hold.** `hello` carries the client's scope. A client that sets one the daemon was not started with gets `scope_mismatch` (-32039) and a hint to run `phonedriver daemon stop`; it is not restarted automatically, because that would end other sessions' work and could start the daemon without the scope. A client without one uses the daemon's scope.

**Evidence.** `crates/cli/tests/app_scope.rs` (21 tests, 15 of which failed with the layer switched off), `core::scope` (8 unit tests), and a run on a Pixel API 31 emulator, `progress.md` 2026-11-20.

**Rejected.** A tool argument or a per-session scope (the model could clear it). An allow-list for `shell` (filtering a command line is guesswork). Blanking screenshots by window bounds. Reading the home screen under the scope. Restarting the daemon on a mismatch.

**Reopen if.** A test needs the share sheet or the home screen; a maker's dialog is let through or withheld wrongly; the runner reports SpringBoard banners, which would let an iPhone take screenshots.

## D103. Off a Mac, a device on Wi-Fi is reached through a remote pairing made once on the cable

**Choice.** Without Xcode (`setup::XCODE` false), `phonedriver ios wifi on` on the cable does what D47 does and also pairs with the device's `remotepairingdeviced`: it starts the lockdown service `com.apple.dt.remotepairingdeviced.lockdown`, runs idevice's `RemotePairingClient` pair-setup through it with the code `000000`, and keeps the record (`RpPairingFile`, which holds this computer's key pair, the device's `altIRK` and, from 0.1.7, the device's public key, D106) in `<home>/ios-wifi-pairing/<udid>.plist`. A record that still verifies is reused. `ios wifi off` deletes it.

- **Finding the device.** `lan::Lan` also browses `_remotepairing._tcp` off a Mac. An announcement belongs to the device whose record's `altIRK` reproduces its `authTag` (idevice's `PeerDevice::validate_auth_tag`); its port goes into `Link::Network { pairing }`. The device itself is still found by its `_apple-mobdev2._tcp` announcement and the cable pair record, as in D47. A one-shot command waits for both, up to the browse's first 2 s.
- **Reaching the services.** `transport::Services` is where a device's developer services answer. It is lockdown on the cable. Off a Mac on Wi-Fi it is the tunnel of the remote pairing (`remote::tunnel`: pair-verify only, which before 0.1.7 did not check that the peer was the device and now checks its signature, D106; `createListener`, TLS-PSK CDTunnel, the userspace TCP stack, RSD), where the lockdown services are reached as their `*.shim.remote` RSD services. A device on Wi-Fi with no record fails with `NeedsUser(PairForWifi)`. Status (amfi, image mounter), revealing the Developer Mode switch, the mount (idevice's `mount_personalized_rsd`, with the chip id from lockdown, which still answers over Wi-Fi), the app list and finding the runner all go through it.
- **Starting the runner.** idevice's `XCUITestService::run` reaches testmanagerd only through lockdown's `CoreDeviceProxy`, which the device closes over Wi-Fi. Coreho's change to idevice adds `XCUITestService::run_over_rsd` (commit `3bd5d45` on idevice v0.1.68: `run` and `run_over_rsd` share the session steps). phonedriver depends on that commit through the copy `hridaya423/idevice` (branch `phonedriver-run-over-rsd`, and from 0.1.7 `phonedriver-verify-device`, D106), kept under the project's own account so that a build does not depend on a contributor's repository; it goes when idevice releases the function. The runner was then reached at port 27753 on the device's address, as in D47, in plain TCP; from 0.1.7 it is reached at that port inside the tunnel (D106).

**Why.** D47's Wi-Fi path needs Xcode's pairing for the runner, the app list and the setup state, so off a Mac a device needed the cable (D96). Spike W1 (`docs/spikes/ios.md`, 2026-11-20) showed that this pairing reaches everything the cable does: on the cable the device answered pair-setup without asking for consent and showed nothing; the fixed code was accepted. Over Wi-Fi the tunnel's RSD listed 83 services, among them testmanagerd, dtservicehub and the lockdown shims, and the runner started through it. The user chose to carry the idevice change in a fork pinned by commit over copying idevice into this repository (2026-11-20).

**Rejected.** Copying the patched idevice into `vendor/`: about 140 files of someone else's code in this repository. Reimplementing the XCTest launch in phonedriver: idevice's session steps are private and run to about 1,500 lines. A custom `IdeviceProvider` that sends `CoreDeviceProxy` through the RSD shim: it would have to fake lockdown's `StartService` answer.

**Evidence.** iPhone17,5, iOS 26.5, Windows 11, home Wi-Fi, 2026-11-20, release build of this change. On the cable, `phonedriver ios wifi on` paired and wrote the record in 1.2 s. With the cable out (usbmuxd listed no device): `devices` listed `ios:<udid>  <name>  26.5  runner`; `doctor` read Developer Mode on, the image mounted and the runner installed, all through the tunnel; the first `snapshot`, which started the daemon and the runner, took 10.7 s, later ones 339 to 450 ms; `screenshot` 408 ms (looked at: the Claude app as it was on the phone); `apps` 963 ms, 151 lines; `launch com.apple.Preferences` 2.96 to 4.71 s; `tap Cellular` 2.16 to 2.69 s, `ok tapped @e45 "Cellular" · new screen`; `press back` 1.63 to 1.79 s via the nav button. Tests: `cargo test --release -p phonedriver-ios -p phonedriver-core --lib` 227 and 90 passed, `-p phonedriver --no-default-features --lib` 105 (with `PHONEDRIVER_AGENT_JAR` pointing at a placeholder, because the Android helper's build tools are not on this computer); clippy `-D warnings` clean on `phonedriver-ios` and `phonedriver-core` (all targets) and `phonedriver --no-default-features`.

**Limits.** One iPhone, one iOS version, one network. The remount over Wi-Fi after a restart (`mount_personalized_rsd`) has not run on a device. How long the record stays valid, and whether it survives an iOS update, is unknown. Right after the cable came off the first `devices` saw nothing; the device announced itself on Wi-Fi a few seconds later. The `_remotepairing._tcp` identifier changed between two announcements on the same day, so matching is by `authTag`, never by identifier. Each new tunnel costs about 0.3 to 0.5 s; the daemon keeps the one the runner's session opened for the app list. On a Mac nothing changes. The record is a key to the device's developer services on the network; it is written readable by its owner only on Unix, and under the user's profile on Windows.

## D104. The guard checks every touch and enter a verb sends, and an approval covers what the human saw (2026-11-21, GHSA-25p2-p3fx-5hmm, GHSA-rm5p-9fp9-hgrx)

**What was wrong.** D27 checked `tap` and `type --submit` only. An outside review showed a guarded action going through without a prompt and without `autonomous`: `type x --into "Pay €49.99"` and `clear "Pay €49.99"` sent their focus tap to the button; `type --into IBAN --submit` on a form whose Pay button is disabled until the field is filled checked the form before the text went in and found nothing; `press enter` after typing sent the form; and over MCP a `swipe` with `distance: 0` from "Place order" is one finger down and up at one point, a tap. A "Slide to pay" control, common in payment and ride apps, was never checked at all.

**Choice.** (1) A finger that goes down on a guarded element and lifts inside it commits it, however it moved: Android delivers the click on the lift while the finger stays in the view's bounds, and a browser does the same within its slop. That holds a zero-length swipe, a one-pixel drag, a one-finger `gesture` and a pinch on the button. (2) A control that commits by moving along it (a slider, or a label with a slide word: `slide`, `swipe`, `desliza`, `glisser`, `wischen`, `scorri`, `veeg`, `स्लाइड`, `スライド`, `滑动`, `밀어서` ...) is held wherever the finger lifts; the classifier reads slider wordings (`Slide to pay`, `Swipe to confirm`, `Zum Bezahlen wischen`, `滑动支付`, `밀어서 결제` ...) as payments and `Slide to send` as a send, and a slider role can now be guarded. (3) `drag` also counts where it lifts, so dropping an item onto a held `Delete` asks. (4) `type --into` and `clear` check their focus tap like a tap. (5) `type --submit` counts a guarded button in the field's form whether or not it is enabled yet, and with no screen read yet in the session it reads one. (6) `press enter` checks the focused element: a focused guarded button, or the focused field's form; with nothing focused and the keyboard up, the whole screen. (7) A touch on an element that takes no touch of its own (a price inside a "Place order" row) counts as the nearest ancestor that does. (8) An approval carries the label and screen lines the human was shown (`approved` on `act` and `batch`); if either changed before the action runs, the human is asked again. (9) Over MCP, `swipe`, `scroll`, `press` and `type` with empty text (clearing a field) ask the human like `tap`; they accept `autonomous`, left out of `tools/list`. On the CLI, `swipe`, `drag`, `pinch`, `gesture`, `scroll`, `press` and `clear` take `--autonomous`, and the hint says "run the same command again". (10) The classifier also reads `Pay with ...`, `Pay` followed by a bare number, and more currencies (`kr`, `zł`, `AED`, `SEK`, `PLN`, `₺`, `₽`, `฿`, `₱`, `₫`, `₪` and others).

**Cost.** An ordinary swipe, scroll or pinch reads the screen no more often than before: the check uses the element the verb resolved, the screen the verb already read (`scroll`), or the session's last screen when it is sure. Only a gesture shaped like a tap (a finger that moves less than 8 points) or a drop onto a point reads the screen when none is in hand. `press enter` and `type --submit` use the session's last screen when it is sure. A test (`an_ordinary_swipe_or_scroll_reads_the_screen_no_more_than_with_the_guard_off`) counts the reads.

**Why one rule per finger, not one check at the device.** A check in front of `Device::gesture` and `Device::key` would see coordinates and key codes, not the verb's resolved element or the form a field belongs to; it would need a screen read for every scroll. The verbs that touch the screen are listed in `act_now`, each calls the guard before it sends, and `tests/guard.rs` holds a case for every one.

**Limits.** A swipe that crosses a full-width guarded button and lifts inside it is held, as the device would click it. A gesture is checked against the screen the session last saw; a guarded button that appeared since, under a moving finger, is not seen. `press fingerprint` on an emulator answers a biometric prompt, which can confirm a payment the app asked for; it is not guarded. The other limits of D27 stand: fixed labels, icons, `shell`, an agent that passes `autonomous` on its own.

**Rejected.** Refusing `type --into` on anything that is not a text field (custom fields report other roles; the focus tap is checked instead). Reading the screen again after the text is typed, before enter (a read on every submit; counting the disabled button covers the reported case). A minimum `distance` for `swipe` (a slider is the honest case, and it is a long swipe).

**Reopen if.** A real app commits on a gesture this rule does not hold (a long press, a two-finger swipe), or users see prompts on swipes that do not commit.

## D105. Secrets and local files in 0.1.7: the mask follows the live list and changed forms, a replayed secret goes only into a password or code field, and what shows the phone is owner-only (2026-11-21)

An outside review of 0.1.6 (advisories GHSA-522g-rv3m-hm5j and GHSA-g453-82w6-qqx5, issue #32) found ways a secret still came back, a replay could type one anywhere, and local files were open to other accounts. Each was reproduced by a test against fake devices before it was changed.

**Choice.**
- **The live list.** `Daemon::call` remembers what a call carries when the call arrives and reads the list again when the reply is ready, so a secret given while another call waits (a `wait log:` answers seconds later) is masked in that call's reply and trace too. The trace masks every remembered secret in the parameters of every method, after its own length rules (an `apps notify` payload, a plain `shell` command).
- **Changed forms.** Besides the text as given, the mask matches it escaped as a JSON string (a raw tree, a fixture) and as a quoted string (a snapshot line, a comparison), with spaces, `-` and parentheses between its characters when it has six or more (`4000 1234 1234 1234`), and the characters just before a `…` that begin it, three or more, where a long text was cut. After a secret `type`, the value the field then shows is remembered too when it holds the typed characters in order in any case, with others between them (`+1 (KQX) WZ7-PL4` for `kqxwz7pl4`), so the field is masked in that reply and in later screens. A matched log line is masked before it is cut to 400 characters.
- **Text made in the client.** A comparison reads text from screenshots in the CLI or the MCP server, where the daemon's mask never ran. The daemon has a `mask` method that answers its text masked; CLI `compare` with one file, MCP `screenshot {compare: true}` and a `replay --screens` failure go through it, and a replay also masks its failure with the secrets it read from the environment itself (those under four characters too).
- **Where a replayed secret goes.** A replay sends a `secret_env` step with `secret_field_only`, and the daemon types it only into a field it sees as a password or a one-time code field (`is_secure` or `secret_field::kind`); any other field, or no focused field, refuses the step before anything is tapped or typed. `PHONEDRIVER_REPLAY_SECRET_ANY_FIELD=1` in the environment of the process that replays lifts it. `{{name}}` no longer fills `secret_env`. A non-`act` step loses the `device` and `share` its file holds.
- **Local files.** `save` writes through a file of a random name created new beside the target, then renames it over the target. MCP `replay` saves only a `.steps` file inside its folder (the server's working directory): no `..`, no link, no parent that a link takes outside, and only over a file that is already steps. A relative `file` is read from that folder too. Screenshots, the folders under `tmp`, a recording's steps file, an Android recording, the trace files and `daemon.log` are created owner-only on Unix (folders 0700, files 0600), and so is phonedriver's home when it is missing. The client connects only to a socket whose folder is this user's and 0700, and only to a process of this user (`SO_PEERCRED` and `getpeereid` through interprocess). The daemon is started without `PHONEDRIVER_SECRET` and every `PHONEDRIVER_SECRET_*` (an explicit environment block on Windows). A secret set as a field's value on Android carries the sensitive mark into the paste it falls back to.

**Why.** D70 promised that a secret is masked in every reply and trace from then on, and the copy taken at the start of a call broke that for a call that waits. D98 gave the `PHONEDRIVER_SECRET_` prefix as the defence against an edited steps file, but the prefix limits which variables, not where they are typed: an agent that can write a file could type every configured secret into a plain field, and from there a screenshot, a comparison or a field that reformats it gives it back. A password or code field does not show its text, and that is where the documented login flow types. Files that hold what the phone showed were readable by other accounts on the machine.

**Rejected.** Refusing `secret_env` over MCP: CI and a person's own MCP setup replay logins that way. Binding a secret to the field it was saved with: the agent can edit the saved target too. Trusting a steps file given on the CLI more than one given over MCP: an agent with a shell runs the CLI, and a person's CI flow types into a password field anyway. Blanking a typed field's value by its ref in every later screen: refs belong to a session, and the verification after `type` reads that value. Matching separators for short secrets: a four-digit PIN would mask `12-34`. Masking any one character before a cut: `Loading…` would lose its `g` whenever a secret starts with one. `PHONEDRIVER_HOME/steps` as a second save folder: a replay reads relative paths from one folder, and two would need a rule for which. Changing the mode of a home folder that is there: `PHONEDRIVER_HOME` may be a folder the person made. Asking the daemon to mask in a two-file `compare`: that compares two files on disk and may run with no daemon.

**Evidence.** `progress.md`, 2026-11-21: each item's test failed first with the leak or the write the report describes, then passed.

**Limits.** Pixels are unchanged: a screenshot, a recording and a kept `--screens` picture show what the phone shows. A two-file `compare` is not masked. Text recognition can read a secret wrong, and a misread one is not matched. A secret the device changes in another way than case, spaces, `-`, parentheses or added characters (a field that drops characters, cut short by the field itself) is still not matched, nor is a cut that leaves fewer than three of its characters. A field that shows the secret changed is remembered only on the device and daemon that saw it. The peer check runs on Unix; a Windows named pipe is not checked. An iOS Simulator recording is written by `simctl` with the default mode. On Windows the files keep their default access. The Windows environment block was built and checked on the Linux build only through the shared filter; it needs a Windows build and a run.

**Reopen if.** A login flow needs a replayed secret in a field that is neither a password nor a code field and the opt-in is too broad; masking a reformatted value hides something an agent needed to read; the socket folder check refuses a setup that is safe (a shared folder with ACLs).

## D106. Only the host that started the iOS runner drives it, the Wi-Fi pairing checks the device, and off a Mac the runner goes through the tunnel (2026-11-21, GHSA-jg7g-xh43-x972, GHSA-mfp4-j74c-44xh)

**Choice.**

- **A token per runner start.** Every start (Simulator, a device through `xcodebuild`, an installed runner through testmanagerd) gets 32 bytes from the OS (`getrandom`) as 64 hex characters in the runner's environment: `TEST_RUNNER_PHONEDRIVER_TOKEN` for `xcodebuild`, which hands it on as `PHONEDRIVER_TOKEN`, and `PHONEDRIVER_TOKEN` in the installed runner's XCTest configuration. The first frame on a connection must be `hello` with a `nonce` (16 random bytes as hex) and `proof`, HMAC-SHA256 under the token of `host:<nonce>`. The runner (`Auth.m`) compares it in constant time (`timingsafe_bcmp`), refuses a nonce it has already accepted, and answers with its own `proof` of `runner:<nonce>`, which the host checks the same way (`hmac`'s `verify_slice`). A connection that fails gets one `unauthorized` reply and is closed; it does not replace the connection being served. Before the check a frame may be 4 KB. A runner started without a token serves nobody. The token never crosses the connection. The wire version is 0.2.0, so a 0.1.6 runner is told apart by its `hello`.
- **Reuse.** The token is kept in `<home>/runner/<udid>.token` (owner only on Unix, written through a rename). A running runner is reused only when it proves that token: `udid` and the build hash are public. So the next daemon of the same home still reuses a runner that a killed daemon left (architecture, Simulator lifecycle), and nothing else is reused.
- **The runner through the tunnel off a Mac.** On Wi-Fi off a Mac (D103) the runner's port is opened inside the remote pairing's TLS-PSK tunnel (`runner::DevicePort::Tunnel`, the adapter's `connect(27753)`, at the device's address in the tunnel), as RSD is. On the cable it is usbmuxd, as before. On a Mac over Wi-Fi (D47) it is still plain TCP to the device's address.
- **The pairing checks the device.** In the idevice copy (`hridaya423/idevice`, branch `phonedriver-verify-device`, commit `f580a6f`, on `3bd5d45`): pair-setup keeps the device's Ed25519 public key from M6 in the record (`device_public_key`), and pair-verify decrypts M2's `EncryptedData` (nonce `PV-Msg02`) and verifies the device's signature over its ephemeral key, its identifier and the host's ephemeral key before the host signs M3; it fails closed (`DeviceNotVerified`, `NoDeviceKey`). A record without the key, which every record before 0.1.7 is, is refused before any connection with `NeedsUser(PairForWifi)`. `phonedriver ios wifi on` on the cable then pairs again and keeps the key.
- **TSS.** The ticket request goes to `https://gs.apple.com/TSS/controller?action=2` and a redirect is an error, not followed.
- **The pairing record file.** The udid must be letters, digits and `-` before it names a file (the token's file too); the record is written to a new file and renamed over the old one, not written when it did not change, and an existing file is set to owner only.

**Why.** Two reports (GHSA-jg7g-xh43-x972 Part A; GHSA-mfp4-j74c-44xh items 2, 3 and 4), checked against the code: on a device the runner listened on all interfaces and served the first frame of any connection, a new connection cancelled the current one, the host reused whatever answered `hello` with the right udid and build hash, the D103 runner channel was plain TCP beside an encrypted tunnel, pair-verify never read M2's `EncryptedData`, and the TSS request was plain HTTP that followed redirects to any host with its body.

**Rejected.** Sending the token itself in the first frame: on a Mac over Wi-Fi the stream is plain, so a listener on the network would learn it and connect. A token held in memory only: a daemon that was killed would leave a runner the next one could not reuse, and on a device a second start while the first runner still holds port 27753 has not been tried. Trusting the first device key seen on Wi-Fi for an old record: the cable is the channel this computer trusts, and on Wi-Fi the first answer can come from anyone. The Mac's CoreDevice tunnel for the D47 path: it exists only while `xcodebuild` or `devicectl` holds the device (spike notes, 2026-11-06), and whether the runner answers at the device's tunnel address through it is unknown without a device. Encrypting the runner channel in the runner itself (TLS with a key from the environment): more code in the runner than this release needs, for the one path that keeps plain TCP.

**Evidence.** `phonedriver-ios` unit tests with a fake runner (the token never in the frame, another token refused, a listener with the public values only not used, reuse only with the kept token, HMAC vectors shared with the runner's tests) and a fake tunnel (the SYN for port 27753 goes into the tunnel, to the device's tunnel address); `ios-runner/tests/test_server.py`, which compiles `Server.m`, `Auth.m` and `Json.m` for macOS and connects over loopback: 9 tests, all 9 failed against the 0.1.6 `Server.m` and pass now; `xcodebuild build-for-testing` for `generic/platform=iOS Simulator` and `generic/platform=iOS` on Xcode 27.0; idevice unit tests with a fake device side for pair-verify (signed by the paired key, another key, other ephemeral keys, no `EncryptedData`, no key in the record) and a local server that answers 307 for TSS. `progress.md`, 2026-11-21.

**Limits.** Not run on a device: a runner on a phone answering inside the tunnel (the `0.0.0.0` listener took IPv6 on macOS, the tunnel's family; not seen on iOS); a real device's M2 verifying against its own M6 key, which neither pymobiledevice3 nor go-ios checks (pymobiledevice3's source says it failed to verify M2's encrypted data, without saying why), so a device whose M2 differs from HAP's would fail every Wi-Fi tunnel with "did not prove it is the device"; the TSS request over HTTPS (a `HEAD` answered 401 with HSTS); a second daemon reusing a runner on a device. On a Mac over Wi-Fi, screens and typed text, `--secret` text included, cross the network unencrypted, and the token stops other machines from opening a connection, not from reading this one or, on the path, from taking it over. The token sits in `xcodebuild`'s environment and in the home, which processes of the same user can read. Pair-setup's M6 signature is not checked: it arrives over the cable.

**Reopen if.** A device run fails any item above; the D47 path can reach the runner through a tunnel phonedriver can open; idevice upstream verifies M2 itself.


**Update 2026-11-22 (audit), reverted 2026-11-23.** For a day a secret was refused on the plain Wi-Fi link (a Mac reaching the runner's port over TCP, which nothing encrypts). That left no way to sign in on an iPhone over Wi-Fi, and an agent refused there could type the same password without `--secret`, which crosses the same link and lands in the trace as well. A secret goes over that link again, as the reference and the iPhone guide say it does; the cable (usbmuxd) and the remote pairing's TLS-PSK tunnel are encrypted. Reopen if the runner's port gets a transport phonedriver can encrypt.
## D107. The app scope and the device list hold where 0.1.6 let them slip (2026-11-21, GHSA-7qw6-v72h-4gvp, #32, GHSA-g453-82w6-qqx5)

An outside review of 0.1.6 found places where D101 and D102 did not hold, and three Android items next to them. Each change below is the safe default; none has an opt-out, because each closes a way around a setting the person made.

**App scope (D102).**
- `open` under a scope: an Android `intent:` URL with `component=` or `SEL` is refused before it reaches the device, because Android starts a named component whatever package is set. The agent (`Apps.openUrl`) also refuses an `open` with an app named when the intent's component is in another package, it has a selector, or the activity it resolves to is in another package, so the handler is the app the scope checked. On an iPhone, iPad or Simulator `open` is refused under a scope: the runner and `simctl openurl` open a link in its default handler, and phonedriver cannot show that the handler is the app in the scope (a custom scheme can be claimed by several apps, a universal link may go to Safari). `launch` opens the app.
- A tree with no window of an app in the scope is outside (`AppScope::redact` adds the whole screen to `outside`), so an empty tree no longer lets input and screenshots through. The agent returns such a tree after its retries, and a dark screen looks the same.
- `page` reads the app the check approved: `Device::page` takes the app, and `ScopedDevice` passes the in-scope application window's package. The Android backend then looks only at that app's own sockets, so a switch of the front app between the check and the read cannot return another app's page.
- A bar is the system UI's (`com.android.systemui`) bar-shaped window, in the agent's `isEdgeBar` and in `core::scope::is_bar`. Android reports another app's overlay window as a system window too, and before this a bar-shaped overlay was dropped by the agent and let through by the host.
- On iOS, `back` and `hide_keyboard` tap the front app's back button or keyboard key, so `ScopedDevice` checks the tree first and refuses while an app outside the scope is in front (`press home` leaves it). Android's are system keys and pass as before.
- The Simulator's `log stream` predicate is `processImagePath CONTAINS "/<container UUID>/<name>.app/"`, from the path `simctl get_app_container` prints, so two apps with bundles of the same name are told apart.

**Device list (D101).**
- `Adb::hardware_serial` fails on an empty answer or a non-zero exit, so a deny list hides the phone, an allow list does not admit it, and `AdbSource` does not keep the answer.
- `Gated::off` filters the off list, and `Gated::boot` matches the name against it before calling the source, so a hidden Simulator is never started. An AVD's id exists only once it runs, so before a boot only a deny entry `avd:<name>` hides it, and the started emulator is judged by its id as before.
- `Gated::list` passes the deny entries to the source (`DeviceSource::list_except`), and `AdbSource` drops a device a deny entry names by id before it reads its Android version or serial.
- A `NoDevice` error from a partly failed lookup has its `connected` and `off` lists filtered by the same rule.
- `doctor --device` probes nothing when the daemon could not answer `hidden` (a device list file with an error): the named device may be the one it hides.

**Android (#32).**
- On a physical phone, `shell`'s refusal for `pm clear` and `pm uninstall` passes only packages that `installed_by_us` accepts, which compares the install time, so an app its owner installed again under the same id is refused, as the typed verbs did in 0.1.6.
- `wait vpn:` takes a dump that did not list the networks for no answer and keeps reading; the VPN's user ids and underlying networks are read after the last `bypassable=` of its `TransportInfo`, past the session name the app chose.
- `GlRelay.draw` makes its own context current, texture and program are deleted only with their own context current, and the shared default display is terminated only by the last relay. Two relays (the main stream and a hidden display's) share one thread.
- The owner file (D34) holds a hash of the computer's name instead of the name. Removing the file when the daemon stops the agent was not chosen: adbd ends the agent when the daemon's stream closes, a crash or an unplugged cable leaves no step that could remove it, and the daemon whose name is in the file is the one that reads it.

**Recordings (D59).** `record start` asks the agent for a sync frame (`sync_frame`, `MediaCodec.PARAMETER_KEY_REQUEST_SYNC_FRAME`) and the file opens at the first keyframe after the start, with an empty edit (`edts`/`elst`) for the wait so the steps file's times still match the video. The units kept since the last keyframe go into the file only when pictures keep coming for `KEYFRAME_WAIT_US` (1 s) with no keyframe, an encoder that ignores the request. A still screen does not wait: the agent asks its encoder for a keyframe at the first repeat after a change (`Video.wrote`; the encoder repeats the last picture for about a second, `KEY_REPEAT_PREVIOUS_FRAME_AFTER`, and an idle screen otherwise gives it no input to make a keyframe from), so the kept group of a still screen is one keyframe and its repeats, stamped exactly 100 ms apart. `stream::still` checks that shape and starts the file from that group, the picture on screen at the start and nothing else; any other group waits as above. Test `a_recording_of_a_still_screen_opens_with_that_screen`.

**Docs.** `site/content/docs/no-telemetry.md` lists the router probe of `pair` and `untether` on macOS (D30).

**Rejected.** An allow-list of `intent:` fields (the URI format has many ways to name a component, and refusing the two that do is exact). Accepting only universal links on iOS (iOS decides their handler at open time; Safari opens them when the app is not set up for the domain). Checking the handler after an `open` (the other app would already have started). Removing the owner file on stop (above). Keeping the cached group as the start of every recording (the privacy cost the review measured) or trimming it (a group cannot be cut without the keyframe it decodes from). An opt-out variable for any of these.

**Limits.** None of this ran on a device. The Java changes (`Apps.openUrl`, `isEdgeBar`, `GlRelay`) compile and are proven by reading only; the host side is proven by fakes. Whether an encoder honours the sync request on an idle screen, and how long it takes on a physical phone, is not measured; an idle screen gives a video that starts blank until the screen changes. Players' handling of the empty edit was not checked. A system UI under another package name (a maker's own) loses its bars to `outside`, which refuses screenshots under a scope on that phone. A screen that shows only a system dialog and no window of the app in the scope is outside. The Simulator predicate assumes the process image path holds the container's folder, as `get_app_container` prints it. `list_except` covers `devices` and listing; `hidden` still enumerates every device to count them.

**Reopen if.** A device run shows a sync request that never brings a keyframe on a changing screen, or a maker's system UI with another package; a player shows the empty edit wrongly; a test needs `open` on iOS under a scope (then a check of the handler before the open is needed).

## D108. `shell` stays on the device unless the person allows the computer when the daemon starts (2026-11-21, GHSA-mfp4-j74c-44xh item 1, GHSA-jg7g-xh43-x972 Part B)

**Choice.** D6 and D21 say agent-written commands run on the device, never on the computer. Two paths broke that, and both are closed by default:
- **iOS Simulator.** A Simulator's programs are processes of the Mac, run as the Mac's user, and reach the Mac's files (a reviewer wrote a property list to `/private/tmp` with `simctl spawn <udid> defaults write <path>`). So `simulator::shell_args` refuses a program other than `defaults` and `log`, and the `simctl` forms that run a program or name a Mac file: `spawn`, `install`, `addmedia`, `push`, `io screenshot|recordVideo`, `keychain add-cert|add-root-cert`, `launch --stdout|--stderr`, and `openurl` with a `file:` URL. What acts on the Simulator alone is unchanged: `ui`, `openurl`, `privacy`, `location`, `status_bar`, `launch`, `terminate`, `uninstall`, `listapps`, `appinfo`, `get_app_container`, `pbcopy`, `pbpaste`, `keychain reset`, `io enumerate|poll`. The refusal says why and points to the verb that does the same safely (`install`, `apps notify`, `screenshot`, `record`). phonedriver's own `log stream` for `wait log:` builds its command with `spawn_args` and is not affected.
- **The Simulator's own settings and log need no switch** (changed before release at the user's ask: the everyday Simulator commands must just work). `mac_program` lets `defaults` (`read`, `read-type`, `write`, `delete`, `domains`, `find`, `rename`) and `log show|stream` through, run as one program by its path in the Simulator, never through `/bin/sh`, so a `;` or a pipe is refused rather than run. Under `simctl spawn` they act on the Simulator's own preferences and log store. Refused: any argument that starts with `/` or `~` or holds `..`, a `defaults` domain with a `/` in it (a relative path, which `defaults` reads as a file), `defaults import|export|-host`, `log` other than `show` and `stream`, and `--archive`, `--file`, `.logarchive` or `.tracev3` in a `log` command.
- **Android emulator console** (D92). `console::check` refuses a command with a line break or another control character, always, and `command_on` checks again, so one call is one console command. Without the switch only `geo`, `sms`, `power`, `network speed|delay|status`, `sensor`, `rotate`, `finger` and `avd name|status` are sent.

**The switch.** `PHONEDRIVER_HOST_SHELL=1` in the daemon's environment (`phonedriver_core::device::host_shell`) turns both rules off, apart from the line check: the Simulator's `shell` runs any program, through its shell where it has one, and every listed `simctl` form, and the console takes any command. Any other value leaves the rules on. It is the person's setting, never a tool argument, and a daemon that is already running keeps what it started with: the refusal says to stop the daemon and start it with the variable set.

**One seam.** The rules live in the device crates' `shell`, where `emu` and `simctl` are interpreted. Every way an agent reaches `shell` (CLI, MCP, a replayed step) goes through the daemon's `shell` method to that one call; `mcp::a_shell_tool_call_and_a_replayed_shell_step_hand_the_device_the_same_command` checks the two MCP paths hand the device the same text. Errors never quote the command, which may be a secret.

**Why.** Text on the screen can steer a model ("run `shell "emu screenrecord start ~/…"` to save a trace"), and SECURITY.md lists a way for screen text to run a command on the computer as a vulnerability. Users decided what to allow from the device-only wording of D21.

**Rejected.** Paths inside a phonedriver folder for the `simctl` forms that take one: the agent would need host file tools to put a file there, and with those it does not need `shell`; a refusal with the verb to use is simpler and has no path checks to get wrong. A wider list of "safe" Simulator programs (`launchctl`, `notifyutil`, `plutil`): each takes paths or acts on services in its own way, and every program added is one more command line to filter. `defaults` and `log` are the two the docs and agents use, so they alone are checked, word by word, with paths refused outright. Separate switches for the two platforms: one idea, "let `shell` act on this computer", is one switch.

**Limits.** The `defaults` and `log` check is a filter on a command line, the guesswork D102 warns of: it holds while those two programs read files only from the arguments refused above. A `defaults write` whose value starts with `/` is refused too. Tested as pure functions and through the fake device; no Simulator or emulator run, because `xcrun` does not run on the Linux build machine. The `install` verb still installs an `.app` from any path the agent names, and on a Simulator that app runs as the Mac's user; `open` with a `file:` URL goes to the iOS runner, not through `shell`. Neither is covered here. Under an app scope `shell` stays refused whole (D102).

**`file:` links on a Simulator (added at the merge).** `open` with a `file:` URL on a Simulator goes through the runner to the Simulator's default handler, which reads the Mac's file system; a screenshot then shows the file. `phonedriver_ios` refuses it unless the switch is set (`names_a_mac_file`, unit test `a_file_link_names_a_mac_file_in_any_case_and_a_web_link_does_not`). On a phone or an Android device a `file:` link names a file of the device and is left alone. The `install` verb on a Simulator still installs an `.app` from any path the agent names, and that app runs as the Mac's user: installing the person's own build is the verb's job, so this stays and is named here as a limit.

**Reopen if.** A test needs a Simulator program often enough that the switch becomes habit (then a typed verb for it); Apple sandboxes Simulator processes from the Mac's files; the emulator console adds a command that acts on the emulator and a test needs it.

## D109. A saved run keeps the build of each app it launches, and a replay refuses another build (2026-11-22, #41)

**Choice.** `save` adds `"build": "<version> (<build number>)"` to each `launch` step, read from the device once per app when the steps are saved: Android `dumpsys package` (`versionName (versionCode)`), the Simulator `simctl appinfo` (`CFBundleShortVersionString (CFBundleVersion)`). Before its first step, `replay` reads the build of each app the steps from `--from` on launch, once per app, and stops before any step when one differs, naming both builds. `replay --any-build` (MCP `any_build: true`) runs it anyway. A file with no `build`, and a device that cannot tell (a physical iPhone), replay as before. Daemon method `build {app}` → `{build}`.

**Why.** Issue #41 (giulioc84) checked replayed scripts against the code: a saved file held no version, so a replay ran its steps on a build whose screens it was never saved on, and a step that still matched text could land on a changed screen. The steps were seen to work on one build; that is all a file can promise.

**Rejected.** Reading the build at every `launch`: it would cost a shell read on every launch of every agent, for a value only `save` needs. Refusing a file with no `build`: files saved before 0.1.8 would all stop.

The GitHub Action passes `--any-build`: it installs the build under test before every replay, so a new version code is the point, not a sign of a stale file.

**Cost.** One `dumpsys package` (Android) or `simctl appinfo` read per launched app at `save`, and the same at the start of a replay whose file has builds. None in a session that does not save or replay. The `replay` tool gains one argument (`any_build`).

**Limits.** A flow that does not launch its app (it starts on a screen already open) records no build. A build that changed without a new version name or number (a debug build installed again) is not told apart. A physical iPhone reports no build.

**Reopen if.** Debug builds of the same version are replayed across code changes often enough to need the install time (D90's `installed_at`) as well.

## D110. A saved step keeps the kind of screen it was saved on, and a replay checks it before touching (2026-11-22, #41)

**Choice.** Each saved `act` step holds `"on": {"app": "<package or bundle id>", "over": <bool>}` from the screen the agent last saw before the action: the app in front, and whether a dialog, sheet or alert was over it. `over` is true when the front app window does not span the screen's width (Android draws a dialog as a window of its own, inset from both edges: `real_dialog.json`, `real_permission_dialog.json` and `vpn_consent_dialog.json` all start 28 px in, every full-screen capture starts at 0), when the header lists a system alert, or when an element is a dialog or an alert (iOS alerts and sheets). A replay sends `on` with the step, and the daemon refuses the action before any touch on another app, with a dialog in front where there was none, or with none where there was one; the replay then stops with its usual "do step N yourself" hint. `launch`, `open`, `press back` and `press home` keep no `on`, since they leave whatever is in front; nor does an action after a failed one (D60's `unsure`). The check uses the screen the session holds, and reads one only when the session has none it is sure of. A file saved before this replays as before.

**Why.** Issue #41 checked replayed scripts against the code and found nothing about the screen was checked before a step: a banner or a dialog put another button under a position step, and a saved text such as `OK` matched a dialog the run was never saved on. Its proposal 5 is a signature of the screen, compared before dispatch.

**Rejected.** Ids, roles and text in the signature, as the proposal had them: they change with content (a list with other rows, a counter, an empty state against a full one), so a good replay would stop on screens that are the same screen. App and front window catch the two cases the report and its attack found dangerous, at no read and no false stop on content. A read of the screen before every step: the screen the last step settled on is the one the next step resolves its target on anyway.

**Cost.** About 40 characters a step in the file. No read in a session that does not replay, and none per replayed step while the session holds a sure screen.

**Limits.** A dialog drawn inside the app's own full-screen window (a Compose `Dialog` in some versions, a bottom sheet whose window spans the screen, a web modal) is not seen as one. A file saved on a phone and replayed on a tablet in split screen sees every screen as `over`. A screen that changed inside the same app with no dialog (another page) passes the check; the step's own target is what catches that.

**Reopen if.** A replay stops for a dialog that is the screen itself (an app whose main screen is a floating window), or a replay runs a step on a dialog this misses.

**Update 2026-11-24 (#53).** A page that slides in is not a dialog. On the API 37 emulator with animations on, Android went on reporting the arriving page's window as starting 41.9 points in and ending at the screen's right edge after the settle was over, so the screen the session held said `over`, and 16 of 40 replays of plain Settings steps stopped with "a dialog is in front". Three changes. (1) The header tells a window inset from both edges (`popup`, a dialog) from one inset from one edge only (`partial`: a page on its way in, a sheet at one edge, an app beside a landscape navigation bar). Both still count as `over` for the check, so a sheet at one edge is not missed. (2) A replayed step whose screen differs from the saved kind reads the screen again every 100 ms until it is that kind or 1 s is over, and is refused only then. The reporter's prototype that waited had no dialog stop in 64 replays. A step whose screen agrees reads nothing more, as before; a real refusal now takes 1 s longer. (3) A step done on a `partial` screen is saved with no `on`, like one after a failed action: its `over` would be true for a slide and the replay would then refuse it every time. **Rejected.** Counting a window as a dialog only when both edges are inset, and not waiting: a sheet or popup that touches one edge would pass as no dialog. **Limits.** A page whose slide outlasts 1 s still stops the replay; a step saved on a real one-edge sheet keeps no screen check.

## D111. A batch step runs the way its tool does alone, or is refused by name (2026-11-24, #50)

**Choice.** An MCP `batch` step is read with its tool's own argument struct, so it accepts every argument the tool takes. Three of those did nothing in a step and the reply was still `ok`. Now: `then` on a `tap` or `type` step becomes the taps it names, as the tool does alone (the reply numbers them as steps, as a lone `tap` with `then` does); `timeout_ms` on a `wait` step is that wait's own timeout, in place of the batch's (wire: `{kind: "wait", condition, timeout_ms?}`); `device` on a step is refused before anything runs (`step 2: \`device\` goes on the batch: every step runs on its device`). The command line's `batch` gets the same: `--timeout` on a `wait` step line is honoured, and any other global option on a step line (`--device`, `--timeout` on another verb, `--no-settle`, `--session`, `--json`, `--expect-change`, `--share`, `--display`) is refused by name, where it was parsed and dropped.

**Why.** Issue #50: `{"tool":"press","key":"home","device":"android:emulator-9999"}` pressed home on the batch's device and answered `ok 1 step`, where a lone `press` refuses the unknown device; a `wait` step with `timeout_ms: 800` took 5,360 ms; `tap` with `then` tapped once and said `ok 1 step`. Unknown arguments are refused by name, so an accepted one reads as one that works.

**Rejected.** Refusing all three (the issue's patch): an agent that writes `then` or a wait's timeout in a step means what the tool does with them alone, and a refusal costs it a turn to say the same thing in more steps.
